λαχοῦ τοῦ παρόντος λόγου ἀδιαφόρως ἐχρήσατο τοῖς ὀνόμασι τῶν ὀνείρων καὶ ἐνυπνίων, ἔδοξεν ἡμῖν καὶ περὶ τούτων πλατύτερον τι διαλεχθεῖν. Εἰσὶ γὰρ διάφορα τὰ παρὰ τοῖς παλαιοῖς τεθειμένα τοῖς καθ᾿ ὕπνους φαινομένοις. Πέντε γάρ ταύτων εἶναι τὰ εἴδη· ὧν τὸ μὲν ὀνομάζουσιν ἐνύπνιον, τὸ δὲ φάντασμα, τὸ δὲ χρηματισμὸν, τὸ δὲ ὅραμα, τὸ δὲ ὄνειρον. Καὶ ἐνύπνιον μέν ἐστι μεθημερινῶν φροντίδων ἀπήχημα· τοῦτο δὲ γίνεται ἐῇ τῆς ψυχῆς ὅπως ποτὲ ταραχθείσης, ἢ τοῦ σώματος τι παθόντος· τῆς ψυχῆς μὲν, ὥσπερ ὁπόταν δεσπότης ὀργίζοιτο δούλῳ, οὐ καλῶς λύσας τὰ ἀνατεθειμένα διοικήσαντι πράγματα, ἔπειτα καθεύδων νομίζῃ περὶ τῶν αὐτῶν τῷ δούλῳ διαλέγεσθαι· ὁμοίως ὥσπερ ἐπὶ τῆς θαλάττης ὁρῶμεν γινόμενον, ὁπόταν αὐτὴν θορυβήσῃ μὲν ἀπαρκτίας, ἔπειτα παυσαμένου τοῦ πνεύματος, ὁ τῆς θαλάττης θόρυβος παραμένῃ· σώματος δέ, ὡς εἴ τις δίψῃ πιεζόμενος, νομίζοι τρέχειν παρὰ ποταμοὺς καὶ πηγάς. Φάντασμα δέ ἐστιν, ὅταν μήτε τελέως καθεύδοντος τοῦ ἀνθρώπου, μήτε τελείως ἐγρηγορότος, ἀλλ᾿ ὡς ἐν μεταίχμιῳ τούτων διακειμένου ἀλλοκότους τῇ τε φύσει καὶ τῇ ποικιλίᾳ καὶ τῷ μεγέθει μορφὰς νομίζῃ βλέπειν, ἢ ἀθρόας, ἢ κατ᾿ ὀλίγας, ἄλλας ἄλλως περιφερομένας, καὶ ἢ θόρυβον, ἢ χαρὰν ἐπαγούσας. Χρηματισμὸς δέ ἐστιν, ὅταν ἢ ἄγγελος, ἢ ἱερεὺς, ἢ πατὴρ, ἢ τι τῶν σεμνότητα ἐχόντων προσώπῳ θεαθὲν προσαγορεύῃ, τῶν μὲν, ὡς κίνδυνον καὶ λύπην ἐπιφέρειν
Ac certe pedum. Vinculorum quibus pedes illius C detinentur. Vigilitantis insomnium. Quoniam sapiens et Industrius noster Synesius in hujus libri frequentibus locis, indifferenter usus est nominibus somniorum et insomniorum, visum est nobis de his quidpiam uberius persequi. Sunt enim differentia ea quae posita constitutae sunt apud veteres, de iis quae apparent in somniis. Quinque enim aiunt eorum esse species: quarum prima insomnium nominatur, altera phantasma, tertia oraculum, quarta visio, quinta somnium. Insomnium quidem est diurnarum sollicitudinum effusio: istud autem fit, anima quodammodo conturbata, vel corpore quidpiam passo. Anima quidem, ut cum dominus ad iram D provocatus servo succenset, quod forsilan turpiter et indecenter opera sibi assignata injunctaque dispensarit: deinde inter dormiendum se existimat his de rebus famulum interrogare. Non secus atque in mari cernimus fieri, quando ipsum concitavit ventus septentrionalis, deinceps cessantibus et quiescentibus ventorum flatibus, tumultus maris permanet: corpore autem, ut si quis siti vexatus se putet currere juxta fluvios ac fontes. Phantasma vero est quando neque profundo vehementique somno homo sopitus, nec plane vigilans, sed veluti in meditullio et intercapedine horum constitutus, insolentes ac prodigiosas natura et varietate, necnon magnitudine inusitatas formas se existimat videre, aut promiscuas et acervatim congestas, aut
μελλόντων, ἀπέχεσθαι, τῶν δὲ, ὡς χρηστά κομί- Α ζειν μελλόντων, ἀντιποιεῖσθαι. Visio, quando quod quis postridie visurus est propter exspectationem, præoccupans istud ip- sum simili et immutabili modo videt. Somnium vero, quando obscure, ænigmatice et umbrose alia pro aliis cernimus, idola quædam et simulacra rerum eventurarum conjectu difficilia, et copiosa quisqui- liarunı farragine obteeta, quæ non parva indigent arte experientiaque, ut dignescatur prænuntiata ab ipsis expositio. Haud quippe opus est alio præ- D ceptore ad ea explicanda, sed fas est seipsum sibi- metipsi magistrum ac moderatorem esse, consue- tudine naturalis privatque suæ vitæ institutionis veluti deductrice utendo. Nam hic inter somnian- dum vidit se vinum bibere, postridié in quasdam res infestas et acerbas incidit: ille vero qui neque istud observaverit, contra voluptatibus quibusdam et oblectamentis fit obvius. Proindo somnia cœlestia cornibus assimilat, eo quod cornua in fastigium vigeant tendant [ue], aut quod proxima affiniaque sint animi principatui, quæ etiam serio agere asserit: sed terrestria dentibus elephantis, quia ipsius dentes ventris satietati subserviant: quod quidem velut terrenum ac materiale in confusionis ac mendacii explanationem sumptum est. Al circum flare funduntur Achaides una, Lugentes misere, et tristem solantur, ab alta Missa aquila anseribus quod mortem immiserit C Latram, Vestibulum ante ipsum rursus librata per auras Insedit tectis, quæ summa palatia jungunt, Ac sic vociferans humanis vocibus usa est: Nobilis Icarii proles clarissima, fidė, Nou sopor illud erat, non somnia vana tenebris, Sed felix potius omen, factumque sequetur Vera fides. Numque anseribus portenditur arce Turba procum. Sed ego tibi sum pennata volucris . Unca aquila ante fui, nunc conjunx dulcis Ulysses Adveni nuper, qui cunctis aspera fata Morte procis mittam, et crudelia ſunera martis. Sic ait, atque meus sopor ipse reliquerat artus, Et dulcis somnus languentia membra remisit. Tum vero anseribus stabulis intenta sub arce, Illos aspicio segetem et frumenta resecta Juxta urnam positam densos velut ante solebant, Curpere vescentes commisto murmure rauco. Tunc Ithacus prudens infert sic ore loquutus : O regina, eiquidem vix fas est dicere causas, Quæque alio vergunt, insomnia noctis in umbris Declarasse notis. Quoniam tibi fortis Ulysses Jamdulum incumbit, meditatus atrocia facta. Namque procis cedes, et tristia funera mortis Omnibus impendent, fluitabit et aula cruore. Nullus enim lethum, et Parcarum fata superba Effugiet. Contra tum Penelopea profatur. Sunt equidem, o hospes, insomnia vana figuris, Et gelido quamvis currunt infirma sopore, Maque judicio, certa et ratione vacare, Nam sine delectu quodam volitare videntur: Atque adeo eventu non omnia facta sequuntur Somnia: saepe quiden veniens deludit imago. At non hac credo venisse insomnia porta. Cum in eo exercitu. Hom:crus in Iliade Jovem inducit volentem honorare Achillem ob ea quæ Thetidi promiserat. Ait igitur: Cum Agamemnoni dormienti somnium immisisset Jupiter, jubet ut omnes simul Græcos ad pugnam contra Trojanos educat, ut videlicet coactus Agamemnon Achillem etiam exire ad bellum hortaretur; nam hæc vox cum omnibus Græcorum copiis, univer- sos simulet collectim summatimque comprehendit. Sed Agamemnon cum non posset assequi intelli- gentiam hujus verbi, quod innuit cum omni orma- torum multitudine, erat immemor Achillis, cui D subjiciebatur Myrmidonum phalanx totius exerci- tus bellicosissima. Et hinc mutilum atque imper- fectum mansit Jovis edictum. Unde non potuit urbem expugnando capere juxta illius pollicita- tionem. Eas abjiciamus. Δοκοῦσι μὲν οὖν ἀνάγειν ψυχὰς δι᾿ ἐπᾳδῶν καὶ ἀφυρκισμῶν τινων· φαίνονται γὰρ καὶ ἀποκρίνονται περὶ τῶν ἐρωτωμένων· ἀλλ᾿ οὐκ εἰσὶ τὰ φαινόμενα ψυχαὶ, ἀλλὰ δαιμόνιά τινα, ἃ δὴ καὶ ἀγγελικὰ μὲν τάγματα ἦσαν τὸ πρότερον καὶ φωτεινὰ, εἶτα δι᾿ ἔπαρσιν καὶ κακίαν ἅπαξ τοῦ θείου ἐκείνου ῥιφέντα χοροῦ, κατὰ τὸν περίγειον τοῦτον πλανῶνται χῶρον, τήν τε λαμπρότητα ἅμα καὶ τὴν λεπτότητα ἀποβεβληκότα ἐκείνην, καὶ μεταθέμενα πρὸς τε τὸ ἀμαυβ ρὸν, καὶ πρὸς πάχος διάφορον καὶ πολύτροπον. Φασὶν οὖν Ἑλλήνων τε καὶ Χαλδαίων σοφοὶ τὰ μὲν αὐτῶν εἶναι ἀέρια, τὰ δὲ ὑδραῖα καὶ ἐνάλια, τὰ δὲ χθόνια, τὰ δὲ ὑποχθόνια· καὶ τὰ μὲν αὐτῶν χαίρειν κνίσσαις, τὰ δὲ αἵμασι, τὰ δὲ μολυσμοῖς τισι, τὰ δὲ ἄλλοις, καὶ ἄλλα ἄλλοις. Διὰ γὰρ τοῦτο καὶ χρησμῳδὸς Ἀπόλλων ἄλλαις μὲν θυσίαις τοὺς νεκυομαντείαν μετιόντας, ἄλλαις δὲ τοὺς ὑδρομαντείαν, ἄλλαις δὲ τοὺς ἀερομαντείαν, καὶ ἄλλως ἄλλους. Ἡμεῖς μὲν οὖν τὰ πολλὰ χαίρειν ἀφέντες, ἐπὶ τοὺς λόγους μετίωμεν μόνον τῶν ψυχοπομπῶν


[617] {#pg.149.617}
γόητων. Οὗτοι γὰρ καὶ τῇ νεκυομαντείᾳ χρῶνται. Τούτοις μέντοι καὶ ὁ χρησμῳδὸς Ἀπόλλων παρακελεύεται τάδε ποιεῖν· θυσίας, φησίν, ἐναρίζων Νερτερίων κατάθπατε, καὶ εἰς βόθρον αἷμα ἄλλε· Χεῦε μέλι νύμφαισι, Διονύσοιο τε δῶρα. Αὐτὰρ ἐπὴν εὔξηαι Λισὴ κλυτὰ ἔθνεα νεκρῶν, "Ενθ᾿ δῖν ἄρνειαν ῥέξειν, θῆλύν τε μέλαιναν· Αὐτὸς δὲ ξίφος ἐρυσσάμενος παρὰ μηροῦ Ἠσύαι, μηδὲ ἐᾶν νεκύων ἀμηρήνα κάρηνα, Δίματος ἆσσον ἴμεν, πρὶν Τειρεσίας πυθέσθαι. Λόγον δὲ ἔχει ταῦτα τοιόνδε· μέλι μὲν διὰ τὸ ἀντίπαλον τῆς τοῦ θανάτου πικρότητος· γάλα δὲ διὰ τὸ μέλαν τοῦ σκότους· οἶνον δὲ διὰ τὴν λύπην· ὕδωρ δὲ διὰ τὸ ἐκ τεσσάρων στοιχείων συγκείσθαι τὸν ἄνθρωπον· δῖν δὲ, φησί, κοινὰ πέλει χθονίων καὶ ἐπιχθονίων τὰ τετράποδα· μέλαιναν δὲ διὰ τοῦ τοῦ τόπου σκοτεινόν· ξίφος δὲ διὰ ἔθος ἐστὶ τοῖς χρώμένοις δαιμονικαῖς μαγγανείαις ξίφει κρατεῖν ὁποῖά τινα, μήπως βιαζόμενοι τοῖς ἀφορκισμοῖς καὶ ταῖς


[618] {#pg.149.618}
ἐπικλήσεσιν οἱ δαίμονες κακόν τι δράσωσιν ἐξαίφνης εἰσπηδήσαντες ἐπὶ τὸν ἀφορκίζοντα καὶ ἐπικαλούμενον. Διὰ τοῦτο τοίνυν ξίφη βαστάζειν ἀναγκάζονται, ἵνα δι᾿ αὐτῶν ἐκφοβῶσιν ἐκείνους, καὶ οὐκ ἐῶσιν ἐγγὺς αὐτῶν προσελθεῖν, ἀλλὰ πόρρωθεν ἐρωτώμενοι ἀποκρίνωνται. Ἐπεὶ γὰρ ζωτικὰ μέν εἰσι πνεύματα, ἔχουσι δὲ καὶ πάχος, ὡς ἔφημεν, δεδίασι καὶ αὐτὰ τομὴν καὶ διαίρεσιν, καὶ ἀλγοῦσι πληττόμενα καὶ τυπτόμενα. Καὶ γὰρ ἐν τοῖς τυπτομένοις καὶ ἀλγοῦσιν ἀνθρώποις οὐχ ἡ σάρξ ἐστιν ἡ τὸν πόνον ὑπομένουσα, ἀλλὰ τὸ ζωτικὸν πνεῦμα. Ἢ γὰρ ἂν ἄλγει καὶ χωρισθεῖσα τῆς ζωογονούσης ψυχῆς. Οἱ μέντοι ψυχοπομποί καὶ ψυχαγωγοὶ ταῖς εἰρημέναις χρώμενοι μαγγανείαις καὶ ἐπικλήσεσιν, οἷον τινὰς μεσίτας ἄγουσι τοὺς τοιούτους δαίμονας, εἰς τὸ ἀνελκῦσαι τινῶν τεθνεώτων ψυχὰς ἃς ἂν βούλωνται· μὴ δυνάμενοι δὲ ψυχὰς ἀναγαγεῖν, ἐξ ἀνάγκης λοιπὸν εἰς τὰς τῶν τεθνεώτων ἐκείνων μετασχηματίζονται οἱ τοιοῦτοι δαίμονες μορφάς, καὶ ὁρῶνται καὶ γνωρίζονται τοῖς φίλοις καὶ συγγενέσι, καὶ ἀποκρίνονται πρὸς τὰ ἐρωτώμενα, καὶ πράττουσι τὰ κελευόμενα. Ἐπιστάνται γὰρ πολλάκις εἰς βλάβην χωρῆσαί τινων, καὶ χωροῦσι. Τοιαύτην δὴ βλάβην καὶ ὁ σοφὸς οὗτος Συνέσιος παθεῖν κεκινδυνευκὼς ποτε


[619] {#pg.149.619}
παρὰ τινῶν ἐχθρῶν ψυχοπομπῶν γοήτων πριπε θέαται δ᾿ ὀνείρων, καὶ τὸν ἐκεῖθεν κίνδυνον ἐξῃ φυλαξάμενος. Τούτου τοίνυν μέμνηται νῦν ἐς ἐπαινῶντης καθ᾿ ὕπνον μαντικῆς. Ψυχοπομπῶν. Ἀναγόντων ψυχὰς νεκύων. Ἡ δέ. Καὶ αὕτη. Ἐγρηγέρσει. Ἀνθρώποις Ἅ τε γάρ. Τὰ εἴδη γάρ ἔχει: οἴκοθεν ἡ φανταστική, ἢ ὡς ἃ προεῖπεν, ὅτι τὰ εἴδη τῶν γινομένων ἔχει ἡ ψυχή, ὡς ὁ νοῦς τὰ εἴδη τῶν ὄντων. Τοῖς ἐστραμμένοις. Δύο κόσμους ἀνωτέρω φήσι., ἀμφιλαφῆ καὶ ἀμφιφεγγῆ, καὶ κρείττονα τὴν ἀμφιφαῆ, καὶ ἅμα τὰς ἐν αὐτῷ τάξεις καὶ θείας δυνάμεις, οὓς καὶ θεοὺς Χαλδαϊκῶς ὀνομάζει, ἀκολούθως καὶ τάδε φησίν, ἃ δὴ καὶ ἔστι δηλα, εἰ προεκθέιμεν τὰς Αἰγυπτιακὰς καὶ Χαλδαϊκὰς δόξας, αἷς οὗτος ἀκολουθεῖ. Ὥσπερ γὰρ ὁ Μωϋσῆς κατ᾿ εἰκόνα Θεοῦ γενέσθαι λέγει τὸν ἄνθρωπον, οὕτω καὶ Χαλδαῖοι καὶ Αἰγύπτιοι τὸν πατέρα τοῦ κόσμου καὶ ποιητὴν φάσκοντι σύμβολά τινα τῆς ἑαυτοῦ ἰδιότητος ταῖς ψυχαῖς ἐγκατασπεῖραι. Οὐ γὰρ οἱ ψυχαὶ μόνον ἀπὸ τῶν πατρικῶν, φασί, σπερμάτων, ἀλλὰ καὶ ὑπερκείμεναι πᾶσαι τάξεις ἐβλάστησαν. Ὅθεν καὶ παραινοῦσιν, ᾧ, ἐπειδήπερ οὐκ ἐκ σωματικῶν ὑφέστηκε κράσεων, ἀλλ᾿ ἄνωθεν ἔσχε τὴν ὕπαρξιν ἐκ Θεοῦ, καὶ συγγένειαν ἔχει πρὸς τὰς ὑπερκείμεναι τάξεις, πρὸς ἐκεῖ ναν ἐπεστράφθαι.
quibusdam inimicis praestigiatoribus mortuorum animas elicientibus, pravidi inter somniandum, et illinc periculum statim evitavit. Hujus itaque nunc meminit in laudem divinandi industrie, quae in somniorum interpretationes suas vires exserit. Quas enim obtinet. Anima siquidem fantastica species continet juxta domesticam et peculiarem naturam, eas nimirum quas supra dixit, quia animus species eorum quae fiunt et eveniunt habet, ut mens species eorum quae revera exsistunt. Is qui vergent. Cum hactenus duos mundos assignaverit, unum qualibet ex parte lucidum, alterum undique caliginosum, et omni ex parte fulgentem prestantiorem esse, simulque ordines qui sunt in ipso ac divinas potestates, quos more Chaldaedeos nuncupat: ex consequenti haec etiam dicit, quae certe erunt manifesta, si prius exposuerimus Aegyptiacas et Chaldaicas opiniones, quas idem observat et imitatur. Quemadmodum enim Moses ad similitudinem et imaginem Dei hominem factum dicit, ita Chaldaei necnon Aegyptii mundi parentem et creatorem dictitant symbola quaedam et indicia sue ipsius proprietatis animis inaeveisse. Haud enim animae tantum, ut aiunt, a paternis seminibus principium habent, sed etiam omnes ordines praecedentes exorti sunt, suamque prius originem habuerunt. Unde nos hortantur et commonefaciunt, ut quoniam non e corporeis temperamentis in rerum naturam productus est, sed ex altiori antiquiorique origine substantiam vitamque habuit,


[620] {#pg.149.620}
ἃ χρὴ, καὶ διὰ τῶν ἀναμνήσεων τῶν θειοτέρων λήξων, ἃς παρὰ τοῦ ποιητοῦ καὶ Πατρὸς ἐλάμβανε κατιοῦσα, ἐκεῖσε αὖθις ἀνέρχεσθαι. Τούτοις ἀκολουθῶν καὶ ὁ Συνέσιος φησιν, ὅτι ἡ ψυχὴ σχολάσασα τοῦ θορύβου τῶν ἀγοραίων καὶ ταχυτέρων αἰσθήσεων, τὰ εἴδη, τοῦτ᾿ ἔστι τὰς ἀναμνήσεις, ἃς ἄνωθεν κατιοῦσα δέχεται καὶ ἔχει, ταῦτα χαρίζεται τοῖς θεωρητικοῖς ἀνθρώποις. Ὁμιλεῖ γὰρ ἤδη καὶ ταῖς θειοτέραις καὶ ὑπερκειμέναις τάξεσι, καὶ συγγένεται αὕτη καὶ δύναμις τις ἐκεῖθεν θεοτέρα, ἐξ ἐκείνου δηλαδὴ τοῦ κόσμου, ἣν καὶ Θεὸν ἐγκόσμιον λέγει.

Εἶτα φρονήσει. Φρονήσει: πεπορισμένην ἀρετὴν φησι τὴν ἐξ ἐμπειρίας ἐγγινομένην· Ἤθει δ᾿ ἐγγενομένην λέγει τὴν ἠθικὴν ἀρετὴν τὴν ἐξ ἔθους καὶ διδασκαλίας ἐγγινομένην. Ὁ μέντοι Ἀριστοτέλης ὥσπερ ἐτυμολογῶν, ἠθικὴν καλεῖ αὐτὴν ἀπὸ τοῦ ἔθους. Ἔθος δέ ἐστιν ἐνέργεια, ἦθος δὲ ἡ ποιότης, ἣ διὰ τῆς ἐνεργείας ἐγγινομένη τῇ παθητικῇ ψυχῇ, τοῦτ᾿ ἔστι τὸ τέλος καὶ τὸ ἔργον τοῦ ἔθους. Οὐδεμίαν δὲ τῶν ἠθικῶν ἀρετῶν φύσει ἡμῖν ἐγγίνεσθαι. Οὐδὲν γὰρ τῶν φύσει ὄντων ἄλλως ἐθίζεται· οἷον τὸ πῦρ, ἀνωφερὲς ὂν φύσει, οὐκ ἄν ποτε ἐθισθείη κάτω φέρεσθαι. καὶ τὰ ἄλλα ὁμοίως.
videlicet ex Deo, etiam cognationem et affinitatem habet cum supereminentibus ordinibus. Ad illos ut convertatur idem homo opere pretium est, propter reminiscentiam divinioris sortis ac haereditatis, quam a creatore atque rerum omnium parente descendens accepit, ut illuc rursus ascendat. Hec verba e vestigio sequens Synesius ait, animam quiescentem et ininumen a tumultu vulgarium et crassiorum sensuum, species, id est, recordationes, quas de superas coelestique regione descendens admittit ac tenet, easdem condonare hominibus qui rerum divinarum ac aeternarum contemplationi dant operam. Jam enim commercium habet cum divinioribus ac supereminentibus ordinibus, atque illuc quaedam vis et facultas divinior ipsi familiariter adhaeret, ex illo scilicet mundo; eam porro Deum mundanum vocat.
Sive intelligentia. Virtutem intelligentia seu prudentia comparatam dicit, quae ex usu experientiaque innascitur; sed ex moribus evenientem et ingenitam ait moralem virtutem, quae ex consuetudine doctrinae exoritur. Aristoteles quidem certe quasi veram dictionis originem disserens, moralem nuncupat ipsam a more. Sed ἔθος assuefactio efficacia est, ut ἦθος indoles seu natura, qualitas est, quae propter efficaciam in animo affectibus obnoxio ingenerata est, seu finis et opus assuefactionis. Nullam vero e moralibus virtutibus a natura nobis ingeneratam esse idem ait: nihil enim iis quae sunt secundum naturam aliam ac diversam assuefactionem induit, ut ignis natura sur-

Subsistentia. Sapientes hunc præsentem mundum simulacrum et effigiem æternitatis esse dicunt : sequitur ergo opera quæ eo sunt simulacra esse eorum quæ re ipsa subsistunt et in illo continentur ; non solum hoc, verum etiam imagines et simulacρα quæ invicem ultro citroque alia ex aliis fiunt et contingunt, idola et ipsa, nominant. Horum autem omnium subjectum aerem capacem esse asserunt : veluti exempli causa, sermonem enunciative et articulatim pronuntiatum minimeque truncatum aer excipiens, et seipsum, tanquam speculum quoddam, occulte partiens in multiplices transfert auditiones, quia simili ratione affectus est cum articulorum vocisque juncturarum constantia. Istud ipsum facit etiam ad omnes colores, cunctosque vapores, et ad omnia quæ in mundo facultatibus vim sentiendi obtinentibus


[622] {#pg.149.622}
κἀνταῦθα ὁ Συνέσιος ἀπορρέοντα εἴδωλα τῶν αἰσθητῶν ὀνομάζει, καὶ ἐκροὰς τῶν συνθέτων. Ἀπὸ μὲν γὰρ τῆς καθ᾿ Ἕλληνας πρώτης καὶ ἀρχεγόνου ὕλης, ἀφ᾿ ἧς τὰ στοιχεῖα, τίς ἂν ἐκροὴ, μόνη τῇ ἐπινοίᾳ πλαττομένης; Κατανοοῦμεν δὲ τὴν αὐτῆς ἐκροὴν ἐκ τῶν κατὰ σύνθεσιν εἰδοποιημένων μερικῶν τοῦ κόσμου πραγμάτων. Ταῦτα γὰρ αἳ γίνονται, ἀλλοιοῦνται καὶ φθείρονται. Οὐ κατὰ μόνον δὲ τὸ τῆς ὕλης ῥευστὸν, ἀλλ᾿ οἷον εἰπεῖν συναπολαύουσι καὶ ταῦτα τὰ εἴδη τοῦ πάθους τῆς φθορᾶς ταύτης, καὶ οὕτως ἀρνοῦνται, φησὶ, τὰ ἐν τῷ κόσμῳ πράγματα τοῦ ὄντος ἀξίαν. Ἀξία δὲ τοῦ ὄντος ἐστὶ τὸ ἀεὶ εἶναι · ταῦτα δὲ τοὐναντίον ἀεί οὐκ εἰσὶν, οὐκ ἐφ᾿ οἷς ἵστασθαι δύνανται, ἀλλὰ τὰ μὲν ἐγένοντο, τὰ δὲ γίνονται, τὰ δὲ μέλλουσιν ἔσεσθαι · τὰ γοῦν ἐκ τούτων ἁπάντων ἀπορρέοντα εἴδωλα περισκοποῦσι καὶ διεξαίθουσι πλανώμενα περὶ τὸν ἀέρα · προσαπερείδοντα δὲ, καὶ μονήν τινα καὶ ἀνάπαυσιν εὐρίσκουσιν ἐν τῷ φανταστικῷ τῆς ψυχῆς, εἰδώλῳ ὄντι καὶ αὐτῷ τῆς ψυχῆς. Νοῦ μὲν εἴδωλον ἡ ψυχὴ · ψυχῆς δ᾿ αὖ εἴδωλον τὸ φανταστικὸν πνεῦμα. Ὅσα μὲν οὖν, φησὶν, εἴδωλα ἀπορρέουσιν ἐκ τε τῶν γενομένων, ἐκ τε τῶν γινομένων, σαφῆ καὶ ἀρίδηλα ἀπόλλυνται, ὅσα δ᾿ ἐκ τῶν μελλόντων, ταῦτα δ᾿ ἀόριστα καὶ δυσείκαστα καὶ αἰνιγματώδη. Ὥσπερ γὰρ εἴ τις
subjacent. Ista igitur ac talia etiam hic Synesius effluentia idola rerum sub sensum cadentium appellat, et concretorum fluenta. Etenim primæ primigeniæque secundum Græcos materiæ, a qua elementa orta sunt, solo mentis conceptu creatæ atque composite, quidam fuerit meatus. Consideramus autem ipsius lapsum ex his quæ sunt secundum coagmentationem compositionemque rerum particularium hoc in mundo suam speciem constituentium : hæ siquidem semper nascuntur, alterantur, in aliamque naturam vertuntur et corrumpuntur. Nec vero duntaxat secundum ipsius materiæ fluxionem, verum etiam, ut ita loquar, ejusmodi species fructum simul hauriunt ejusdem affectionis et corruptionis ; et ita, ait, res quæ in mundo continentur, recusant ac repellunt dignitatem ejus quod revera est, veramque habet substantiam. Entis autem dignitas est semper esse. Hæ contra species rerum non semper exsistunt, nec ab uno perstare possunt, sed quædam fuerunt, quædam nascuntur, quædam vero esse debent. Quocirca ex his omnibus effluentia simulacra omnia perlustrant, vagantur, et effugiunt per aerem oberrantia : sed ad aliquid applicantur, huicque incumbunt, et unicam laborum relaxationem in anima : spiritu fantastico inveniunt, qui quidem est idolum ac simulacrum animæ. Mentis quidem idolum est anima, rursus animæ simulacrum spiritus est fantasticus. Quæcunque igitur, inquit, idola effluunt ex his quæ fuerunt, et ex his quæ nascuntur oriuntur perspicue ac dilucide pereunt : quæ vero futurorum sunt, ista incerta, conjectu ardua, obscura et ænigmaticis

Quemadmodum enim si C quis viator præteriens amygdali florem conspexerit, novit continuo quia fructus amygdali florem subsequitur: qualitatem certe, magnitudinem et frugum perfectionem opportunitatemque haud definite novit. Hunc in modum quæcunque simulacra e rebus futuris fluunt, prænuntiant quidem quidpiam eventurum, non tamen ut plurimum aperte rei qualitatem. Imagines. Ut sunt characteres, simulacra et typi. Hypostasi. E sua essentia et natura. Materia. Hoc est, ex compositis fleri. Eo quod decidit ac dilabitur, lapsum facit. Convincit. Quasi superior evadit, et admittit. Transferri. Effluere ex compositis, quantum ad D materiam et speciem, quæ quidem ambo fragilia sunt et caduca. Ejus quod est. Rei quæ proprie est in rerum natura. Quæ eveniunt. Quæ sunt interitui obnoxia, fragilia, mutabilia. Effugientes. A scopo aberrantes. Et incertitudine substantiæ. Cum sint typi ac formæ eorum quæ sunt in rerum natura et umbræ, non tamen exsistunt. Eorum quæ sunt. Quæ reipsa et per se exsistunt. Agnoscantur. Tanquam alienæ et abhorrentes ab illorum natura. In animales. In spiritus imaginationis. In natura. Humana. Atqui at his quæ. jamjam exoritur et emergit, non est ens: neque enim in perfectam suisque omnibus partibus absolutam naturam venit: ita hic etiam comparat Synesius et recenset ea quæ dicit. Exempli causa, haud dubie hominem quandiu vivit, ut rem consistentem considerat: sed posteaquam mortem eum vita commutarit, tunc illum perpendit consummatum, et jam est ens vi occulta, veluti cesset ac desistat ab essentia, et consummationem sumat ejus quod prius potentia erat. Vivaciores. Viventes, superstites. Natura imperfecta. Imperfectum est enim id quod futurum est, propterea quod nondum in originem venit. Exsilientia. A floribus deprompta est hæc metaphora, qui ante fructus exsilient et erumpunt. Inde proficiscuntur, Ab eo spiritu. fantastico. Sed jam aliquid de arte dicendum. Hinc exorditur docere, quo pacto nosmetipsos præparare oporteat ad susceptionem artis divinandi, quæ fit per somnia. Spiritum. Fantasticum. Pastus. Docet qua methodo operæ pretium est enutrire et augere artem divinationis somniorum. Asserit autem id fieri debere per philosophiam, quippe quæ mortis meditatio est, et per molestum et frugale vitæ institutum. Ista enim sunt veluti frena affectuum et appetituum, atque immobilitas quadam. At incomposita, proterva et libidinosa vivendi ratio, fluctuationem, ait, ac tempestatem in extremo corpore ingenerat. Quod quidem ille

Hoc rursum corpus per vicissitudinem movet sensitivas facultates, quæ violentiam usque ad imaginationem transmittunt: ut quando quispiam aquam et aerem turbat et commovet, et rursus quod turbatum est aliud commoverit; et illud aliud, atque servata serie istud diu vicissim fiat. Vide autem quo pacto primum corpus fantasiam seu imaginatricem animæ facultatem nominavit primum corpus. Primum, propter extremum et concharium integumentum; corpus vero, eo quod crassius corpulentiusque sit, et propius ad materiæ naturam accedat. Pastus. Educatio. Et moderatum victum. Philosophiæ, necnon ingenuæ simplicisque vitæ beneficio. Cum corpore extremo. Carnali et belluino, quod concharium vocavit operimentum. Ecs qui mare permeant. Maritimus dicitur qui in ponto vel mari navigat. Sunt enim ipsa veluti prævii præcursores eorum et similia similium. Similia facta nobis plerumque sortito adveniunt. Repentinas pluvias. Hiemes, procellas, imbres, fulmina. Circulos. Tres, vel duos, vel unum. Sidereos circulos. De circulis instar areæ circa lunam apparentibus verba facit. Ex uno quidem. Φησὶν οὖν ὅτι οὐχ οὕτως ἐστὶ δυνατὸν ἐπὶ τοῦ φανταστικοῦ πνεύματος, ὥσπερ ἐστὶ περὶ τοῦ σαρκίνου τούτου καὶ ἐσχάτου σώματος τῆς ψυχῆς καθόλου τέχνην παραδοῦναι, καὶ ἕνα κανόνα τῶν παντοίων καὶ παντοδαπῶς φαινομένων ὀνείρων. Τὸ μὲν γὰρ σῶμα ἢ ὑπὸ ψύξεως· ἢ ὑπὸ θέρμης τριπόμενον πάΦαινόμενα, de his quæ in cœlo conspiciuntur et apparent, multa alia de luna commemorat, atque etiam ab antiquiori superiore principio exorsus: Una, duplex, triplex, si cinxerit area lunam, Unica ventorum præsagia, signa sereni Et feret: abruptos subito prænuntiat Euros Marcescens tenui sensim caligine, et æthra Digestus patula, docet undis adfore pacem. Hujus loci sensus hic est: Si una fiat area, serenitatem exspecta, densitas enim exiguo momento dilabitur. Quod si erumpat, ventum præstolare ex illa parte, unde fracturam sumit: sin autem duæ fuerint areæ, hiemem ac tempestatem sperato, et adhuc majorem si sunt tres. Infimum. Synesius multoties supra spiritum fantasticum primum animæ corpus nominavit, et corpus intemeratum ac divinum: infimum vero corpus concharium integumentum, carnem cum nervis et ossibus reliquisque corporis partibus, quæ simul aluntur. Quod certe etiam elementorum familiaritate conjunctorum congressum vocat, hoc est, eorum quæ ab omnibus ita nominantur: sunt autem calidum, frigidum, siccum et humidum. Ait igitur non ita fieri posse de spiritu fantastico, quemadmodum licet de hoc carnali et infimo corpore animæ generatim artem tradere, atque unam regulam universorum, et quibuslibet malis apparentium somniorum. Corpus siquidem vel a frigore, vel calore incitatum frequenter ita afficitur. At spiritus fantasticus infinitas habet differentias

ac transinulationes, alterationesque, de quibus non pauca supra diximus : hic quidem qui spissus et crassus est assimilatur naturæ terræ, ille vero aquæ, alius aeri terræ propinquiori quantum ad tenuitatem : alius autem aeri super nebulas collocato. Ille porro elementalis ignis circulo, alius æthereo : et alius quidem sphæræ solari, ac deinceps similiter alius alii ; et alter alteri, prout unusquisque animatus et affectus est ad crassitudinem, tenuitatem aut lenitatem, juxta congruam mensuram Corpus infimum. Quæ quidem elementorum est congressio ac societas. Congressio. Non ita se res habet de spiritu fantastico. Et rationem. Solertem artis medendi peritiam hominum qui nondum procreati sunt, nec adhuc vitales auras carpunt. Nam plerumque. Et istud interjectum usque ad ista : non ita calore et frigore. Ultra naturam. Extra consuetudinem. Non occulto corripitur morbo. Quibusdam enim aliquoties contingit imbecillitas recentior et insueta. Gnomonem. Hanc regulam adhibemus. Alius ab alio. Fantasticus a fantastico. Coagmentatæ materiæ. Crassitudinis, spissitatis imateriæ.

C uti in currum inducens universi naturam demonstravit, ipsisque leges fatales narravit : fore quidem ut unica prima origo omnibus imponatur, statuaturque ne aliqua detrimentum percipiat ; et quæ sequuntur. Ei quidem. Spiritui. A scripso. A domestico et familiari instituto. Nihil enim. Neque enim quidquam aliud tam jubricum, procax et ad motum tam proclive quam spiritus ille fantasticus. Ita versatile. Tam motui et perturbationi obnoxium. Ab iisdem. Spectris et imaginibus. Sed quonam modo evenire potest. Obstaculum, D inquit, multiplex est, et error accurate imaginis in rebus quas imaginatione continentur. Etenim diversum in corporibus humanis temperamentum distincte quoque ostendit ea quæ apparent, necnon diversum vitæ institutum, et studium, atque differens educatio, dissidens est etiam ætatis vigor, varia est item commotio rerum quæ imaginatione comprehenduntur et effinguntur. Nam aqua liquida, inquit, evidentiora rerum apparentium demonstrat simulacra, quam aliquo colore imbuta turbataque ; et ipsa turbida juxta varietatem colorum et inquinamenti, varias ostendit imagines. Et rursus eadem aqua manente, ipsa quoque diversa commotio differentiam efficiet apparentium idolorum. Aut enim non omnino spectrum apparebit, aut vix tandem præposterum. Nam ejusmodi commotio

concussit somniorum rectitudinem et veritatem. Propterea, inquit, arduum est in tam variis discrepantibus rebus, simile spectrum commonstrari, arduum præterea regulam tradere. In statu permanens. Non commota. Disponebatur. Informetur, et effigies suscipiat. Crassitudo. Inquinata et turbida, vel potius inquinamentum. Aliud est inquinamentum albi, aliud ejus quod nigrum videtur, aliud denique ejus quod puniceum apparet. In alie. Aqua. In plurimis figuris. Aquæ, aut tortuosis, ac recto tramite directis. Sive. Videtur consuetudinem et colloquium habuisse cum quibusdam horum artificum de imaginatione ac divinandi arte Synesius Melampus. Hic Melampus Ægyptius erat rerum sacrarum et apud eos augustarum scriptor, ac ceremoniarum antistes, necnon augurio clarissimus. Is, ut aiunt, Græcis tradidit Ægyptiorum sacra, ut ea imitarentur, et ipsi ab illo informati iisdem operam darent. Sed Phæmonoe vates erat Apollinis in edendis oraculis versibus hexametris utens. Censebit. Dogmata tradet, præcepta dabit. Ex dispari materia. Sunt enim ex vitro specula, et ex chalybe, et alia materia. Circa spiritum. Vel de varietate spiritus non locuti sunt, sed quasi universum sit unius naturæ totum esse in rebus affirmarunt, quod sit in imaginandi facultate. Esse regulam. Magistram futuræ

Multi enim, somnio lacte potato, vigiliam serenitatem habuerunt, imminentibus tumultuum serenitatem ac tranquillitatem omnes pariter habuere, quamvis inter se dissentientes essent. Rursum vino epoto molestiam perceperunt, simili ratione inter se discrepantes, et infinita alia, non tamen in omnibus id semper fit, sed in aliis atque aliis aliter atque secus. Si dissipetur. In alia aliorum loco. Prius exsilientis. Supponenda res est duobus peractis annis eventura, si hodie in imaginatione apparuerit, prius aufugit ex ordine domestico et peculiari, atque provolutio rei nondum præsentis, ac naturæ imperfectæ efflorescens germen. Difficilius est. Præterea vero, ait, magis arduum est assequi et colligere communem methodum in singulis hominum moribus. Nam hominum cujusque peculiarem quandam consuetudinis habitatem; quod censium, voluntatem et cognitionem, habet: idcirco vario modo phantasmata singulis demonstrantur, et alia aliis aliorum fiunt significativa, nec ipsa ipsorum omnibus: res est igitur plena laboris visa phantasmata colligere, et nosse quæ singulis hominibus distincte eveniunt, et communi spectro compar atque æquale exprimere, hoc est, aliis simile, nec communem unicuique omnibus particulatim hominibus horum dare doctrinam, quemadmodum fortassis sigillatim et separatim a manibus communis animalis rationem ac definitionem assignamus, quod sit substantia

Quod porro juxta uniuscujusque voluntatem, cognitionem, considerationem ac dispositionem, interdum vero juxta eam artem, cujus unusquisque sit particeps vigilando dormiendoque demonstrentur præcognitiones, hinc patet. Nam Assyriorum regi Nabuchodonosor nimirum strenue et alacriter occupato circa variarum pecuniarum inventionem ac possessionem, ostendit Deus futurum per simulacrum auro argentoque exornatum et instructum, et ex illa materia confectum, quæ eidem familiaris erat. Magis præterea toto animo et studio omni in astra eorumque motus et collocationes incumbentibus, per stellam incarnatæ divinæ providentiæ mysterium demonstravit. Et ipsi Abraham in Ægyptum profecturo res futuras indicavit, quæ ipsi eventuræ erant, per artem sibi familiarem et assuetam: ea autem Chaldaica divinatio ex hominum nativitate desumpta, et præscientia et sectione fibrarum hostiarum. Juvencam enim, dicit illi Deus, triennem, et capram trimam mihi sumito, ac duas in partes dividito, et partes bifariam divisas in anteconspectum ponito. Quod sane ubi fecisset, præscivit semen suum peregrinum et accelum fore in terra aliena, et servitutis jugo detentum iri annos quadringentos: et quæ sequuntur.

Ars. Cognitio a familiari et contubernali. Scitum porro. Alio etiammodo scitum et industrium fuerit. Morosa fuerit. Fatue, insolenter ac stupide constituta fuerit. Acta diurna. Hinc manifestum est Synesium scripsisse librum sua somnia et quæcunque eadem subsecuta sunt continentem: ea quippe vocat nocturna commentaria, sive somniorum adnotationes, quæ nocturno tempore eveniunt, et facta quæ eadem consecuta sunt et insuper accesserunt. Utrague. Diurna et nocturna. Media. Quæ neque ad id quod melius, nec ad id quod pejus est, vergit. Enter vigilandum. Ut Aristides orator fecit. Nam cum aliis voluminibus ipse composuit etiam alterum, quod juxta subtilitatem complectebatur sua somnia, quæ valetudine adversa obrutus viderat. Philostratus quidem certe Lemnius, Sophistarum vitam describens, mentionem hujus oratoris facit, atque ista de eo dicit: Aristidem, sive Eudæmonis, id est beati filium, sive beatum, Adriani producerunt. Est autem (Adriani) exiguum Mysorum civitas. Athenæ vero eum instruxerant, qua temporis Herodis florebat ætas, et Pergamum in Asia, juxta stolis vocum obscurorum interpretationem. Cum

Igitur morbi speciem, et quod nervi eidem horrore torpescerent, ipsemet in divinis libris asserit. Sed eos diurnorum actorum libros scripturus sibi quemdam sermonem applicat et accommodat; hæ porro ephemerides bonæ atque utiles sunt magistræ bene disserendi de qualibet re. Hoc utique in loco omnes, in quorum viribus situm est scribere, cohortatur Synesius, ne linguæ ac styli laborem impendant in alia absurda quædam argumenta, verum exercitationem ac speciem adhibeant circa ea quæ inter somniandum videntur, ac talibus utantur pro diurnis commentariis. Memini quoque talio, qualis videlicet commemorat Lemnius Philostratus de Aristide uretore, alacriores promptentesque tali testimonio reddens auditores. Ad elocutionis argumentum. Qui nihil apud se simile cogitarint. Imperitis hominibus. Cum tempore. At phantasia, inquit, ista adferens citra omnem temporis mensuram ostendit, non tanquam multas species, hoc est, velut distinctam habentes varietatem, sed omnes simul ut unam. Atque porrigit. Ipsi phantasiæ largitur. Suspicione ducimur. Imaginamur. De quacunque re vomerit. Nam sæpenumero ostendit divitias, gloriam, aut mulierem speciosam, ita indicans prout quisque, quod valentatem et immodicam sub-etheream, amatus est circa res materiales. Voluerit. Phantasia. Absolutissime. Perfectissimam dicit rhetoricam D antiquam captiosam et cavillatricem Sophistarum artem, quam rhetoricam philosophantem Philostratus Lemnius vocat in Sophistarum vita. Disputat enim, inquit, priscus sophista his de rebus in quibus occupantur ii qui philosophiæ studio indulgent, cum animi fiducia, sicuti peritus Oretionum vero præmia facit : Hoc scio, et, illud novi, istud pridem considerari, et, nihil homini stabile ac certum est. Talis exordiorum species et verborum generositatem resonare facit, et intellectum, vimque animi, ac dilucidam set comprehensionem et inventionem. Adhæc similis est studicie et in exprimene s oraculis perite divinationis. Etenim Pythias Apolpermulta ita expriunt. Mensuram pelagi teneo, numerus et arenæ.

Argumentorum. Quæ in somniis sese offerunt. Sermones. Libros scriptitare. Opinor. Nam et in somniis sibi videntur alia animantia identidem cum hominibus disceptare, arbores suopte conatu et impulsu transmutari, et homines instar volucrum volare, et quantum ad formam figuramque in belluas rationis expertes converti, et alia infinita monstrosa spectra. Proinde asserunt ii qui fabulas composuerunt ab initio ex ipsis somniis habuisse occasionem. Ut enim somniorum licentia et facultas prodigiosas atque insolentes visiones ostendit, imbecillam speciem, et imaginem veritatis ænigmaticis velaminibus opertam, et, ut ita loquar, priorum lineamentorum designationes eorum, quæ inter vigilandum eventura sunt: sic et fabulæ cum licentia quadam affingunt et comminiscuntur plantas, lapides, hecnon bruta animantia humana voce disserere, et complura talia, in quibus latitant adumbratæ quædam descriptiones, et verarum cohortationum seminaria, et voluptatis larvam in rei domesticæ dispensatione avidæ multorum auditione demonstrant. Libanius ea meditabatur, et in his stylum exercebat, totum argumentum in longitudinem et amplificationem extendens, et componens, ac veluti umbratile certamen exercens, et ita contendens. Itaque meminit hoc in loco Synesius unius illorum problematum: et sic se habet: Quidam pauper et alter opulentus, mutuo odio flagrabant: pollicitus est autem dives se civibus alimenta subministratur, si pauper morte plecteretur: concessit populus. At filios pauperis non educavit dives, sed fame perierunt: tanquam in sordibus et in reatu caedis esset, accusatur locuples: talis quaestio meditando exagitatur secundum dictum et sensum. Nonagenarius. Hic sophistam Libanium perstringere videtur, qui ad extremam senectutem progressus opere pretium duxit in declamando studium collocare; nullius enim plures meditationes reperiuntur quam ipsius, nec tot. At meditatio, inquit, adumbrata quaedam est descriptio ac praecedens exercitatio cujusdam operis. Qui autem operis umbram identidem ut opus exercet, imprudenter et occulte mendacium pro veritate apprehendit, et incassum laborat. Preparationem. Umbram. Pancratium. Olympica certamina quinque esse dicunt, cestum, luctam, cursum, jaculum et discum: erat quidem certe aliud etiam pancratium, quod constabat e pugilatu et lucta. Licebat enim ei qui quinquertio uti volebat et in eo se exercere, non modo leges luctae, sed etiam pugilatus vincendi gratia adhibere. Hic igitur Synesius abusive hanc

[643] {#pg.149.643}
FRAGMENTA
EX NICEPHORI GREGORÆ DIALOGO INEDITO CUI TITULUS:
ΦΛΩΡΕΝΤΙΟΣ Η ΠΕΡΙ ΣΟΦΙΑΣ.
(Ex ms. cod. Reg. Paris 2992 edidit Val. Parisot in Cantacuzène homme d'Etat et historien, Paris, 1845. Viri docti analysin vernaculo sermone scriptam retinemus (*)).
Le Florentius est un de ces dialogues dont Grégoras affectionnait la composition et dans lesquels il imite la manière de Platon et de Lucien, mais encore plus celle du dernier, tout en se flattant de reproduire les allures et le style du premier. Son but, dans celui-ci, est de raconter, en l'arrangeant, un colloque publie qu'il eut avec Barlaam en 1330 ou 31, et dans lequel il parait que l'avantage lui resta Il a lui-même fait mention de sa relation de cette espèce de tournois d'érudition dans son Histoire XI, 10, et il assure ne l'avoir écrite qu'à la sollicitation de ses amis. Il y déguise les noms des personnages principaux sous des pseudonymes : ainsi lui-même, il devient Nicagoras (pseudonyme qui présente le commencement de son premier nom et la fin du second) ; on reconnaît Métochite dans Métrodore, c'est probablement Syradène (à moins qu'on ne soupçonne Cantacuzène lui-même) qui est Démérate ; les deux partis (pour Andronic fils et pour son petit-fils), sont nommés Héraclides et Cécropides : de même les noms de lieu (Athènes pour Byzance, Calydon pour Durazzo ? la ville anonyme de Thessalie pour Thessalonique ? etc.) ; et aussi l'ostracisme pour le simple bannissement.—Le dialogue est véritablement très-piquant sous plus d'un rapport ; et il offre de l'intérêt pour l'histoire littéraire ; il est étonnant qu'il n'ait pas été encore publié. On nous saura gré d'en extraire quelques passages qui peuvent, même en atténuant les exapérations de Grégoras, faire bien saisir les deux traits dominants du caractère de Barlaam, une grande outrecuidance et une grande légèreté, ce qui du reste n'empêche pas qu'il ne joignît en réalité à des connaissances sur divers point (sans être encyclopédique comme il le prétendait) de la facilité et de la faconde.
Voici comment Barlaam est mis en scène (après dix ou douze pages de propos relatifs en partie aux affaires politiques).—ΦΛΩΡΕΝΤΙΟΣ. Ἐκώμασέ τις ἐς τὴν Ἑλλάδα Ξενοφάνης ὁ Θρασυμάχου, πολλὴν τῆς σοφίας ἐπίδειξιν ποιήσειν ἐπαγγελλόμενος, καὶ πρῶτα μὲν ἐς Καλυδῶνα πόλιν μεγάλην τῶν Αἰτωλῶν ἐπεδήμησε· κἀκεῖθεν ἐξέστειραν πόλιν μεγάλην καὶ οὐδεμιᾷ τῶν πρωτείων παραχωροῦσαν ἐς λόγους ὁπόσα ἐξ Αἰτωλῶν ἀρξάμενοι, μέχρι καὶ ἐς Λόκρους τῆς Ὀζόλας διήκουσι.—ΚΡΙΤΟΒΟΥΛΟΣ. Πόθεν
(*) Vide etiam tomi superioris col. 782, ubi Dialogi initium legitur. EDIT.


[644] {#pg.149.644}
δ᾿ ὁρμώμενος οὗτος ἦν ὁ ἀνήρ, καὶ ποίας πατρίδος καὶ γένους ; — ΦΛΩΡΕΝΤΙΟΣ. Οἶσθα, ὦ φίλε Κριτόβουλε, κόλπον τινὰ Ταραντίνων καλούμενον, στενόν τε καὶ ἐς πολὺ παρατείνοντα μῆκος, καὶ πόλιν μεγάλην πρὸς τὸ ἀρκτῷον κέρας κειμένην αὐτοῦ Λακεδαιμονίων ἄποικον ; — ΚΡΙΤΟΒΟΥΛΟΣ. Εἰ μὲν ἄλλην τινὰ φῄς, οὐκ οἶδα, Φλωρέντιε· εἰ δὲ τὴν μετὰ τὸν ἰόνιον κόλπον, καὶ μάλα γε οἶδα.—ΦΛΩΡΕΝΤΙΟΣ Οὗτος τοίνυν Ταραντίνων ὁ κόλπος, ὦ φίλε Κριτόβουλε, πρὸς μὲν δύσιν καὶ Ζέφυρον ἄνεμον ἀφίησι τὴν δευτέραν καλουμένην Ἑλλάδα, τὴν τῆς Πυθαγόρου σοφίας πάλαι αἱρετιστῶν· πρὸς δὲ ἕω τὴν Καλαβρῶν ἐπαρχίαν, ἥτις ἐν δεξιᾷ ἰόντι ἐς ἄκραν Ἰαπυγίαν τῷ ἰονίῳ ξυνάπτει πελάγει· ἐξ ἧς καὶ ὁ σοφὸς ἐκεῖνος ὥρμηται Ξενοφάνης. Διαλέκτῳ γε μὴν κέχρηται, φύσει μὲν τῇ Λατίνων καὶ Ἰταλῶν, βεβιασμένος δὲ καὶ τῇ γε Ἑλλάδι ταύτῃ καὶ ἐγχωρίῳ ἡμῖν. Il dut, au reste, avoir d'autant plus de facilité à l'apprendre que non-seulement, comme le remarque Grégoras, son pays natal avait été la Grande-Grèce, mais que le sud de la GrandeGrèce avait appartenu jusqu'au onzième siècle à l'empire grec : il existe encore au fond des Calabres (qu'au reste, nous ne confondons pas avec la Calabre de Barlaam), à Rossano surtout, des vestiges de langue grecque. Il devait y en avoir bien plus dans le premier tiers du quatorzième siècle, quoique déjà l'italien fût formé.
Après diverses interruptions de Critoboul, Florentius (qui sera le narrateur de l'entretien entre Grégoras et Barlaam) nous montre l'enfant de la Calabre voulant connaître la Grèce par lui-même et se hâtant de venir en ce pays où il apprend tolérablement la langue, mais où ses prétentions déviennent démesurées et où l'orgueil lui tourne la tête, surtout après qu'il a quitté ses premières résidences (Calydon et une ville anonyme de Thessalie) pour Athènes. Ἐκεῖνον τοίνυν τὸν Ξενοφάνην, ἵνα ἄνωθεν ἀρξάμενος διηγήσωμαι, γένος μὲν ἐκ Καλαβρίας εἶναι ἠκούσαμεν, ὦ λῷστε Κριτόβουλε, τὴν δὲ σοφίαν οὐκ ἐκ Καλαβρίας, ἀλλ᾿ ἐς τὰ βαθύτερα τῆς Ἰταλίας ἰόντα, ἀρύσασθαι, καὶ ταύτην οὐ παντοίαν καὶ πᾶσαν, ἀλλὰ μόνην τὴν Ἀριστοτέλους ὅση περὶ τὰ φυσικά γίνεται καὶ ὅση περὶ συλλογισμούς, ἐπειδὴ καὶ αὐτή γε ἐς ταμάλιστα τοῖς ἐκεῖ Λατίνοις τε καὶ Ἰταλιώταις σπουδάζεται, οὐ κατὰ
SCHOLIA ON SYNESIUS' "ON DREAMS"
NICEPHORUS GREGORAS' EXPLANATION OF SYNESIUS' TREATISE "ON DREAMS"
PREFACE
It is nothing new or absurd if many things that were found with great difficulty in the past are improved upon, just as from the cycles of time other great and vehement natures sprout and rise up one after another, whether it be that time itself is so ordered that it always brings forth newer things upon the old, or that human nature is unable to reach that which is perfect from the very beginning. For while Isocrates says, "If you are fond of learning, you will be learned," you act in the opposite way. For you were [learned], but you are always becoming so. You are always fond of learning, as if your nature were not satisfied with what has already been achieved, unless it also further proposes material for a feverish thirst, just as if a flame were to seize upon a reed; the more it advances, devouring the fuel, by that much it multiplies its leaping and rises more vehemently against the table beneath it. And if that great Plato, coming forward, demonstrates that the man fond of learning is in a certain way a philosopher—because he embraces sublime things in his thought and whatever mysteries rise from the earth—then nature pursues what is stronger and more excellent, which is left for you. For such are the ardors and loves of philosophy: the more deeply they enter into the reasons of things that exist and insinuate themselves into them, and the more they advance into their very enclosures, by that much do they feed and increase a swelling appetite for even higher things. That, indeed, is something that has sometimes not raised me to the least degree of admiration: in what way, although you see all the Greeks who currently flourish among us, you nevertheless overlook them, and then you do not at all hesitate to entrust to me alone the burdens of your own logical and wisdom-filled questions, nor do you leave them any opportunity for ambitious exercise.
Since it is not fitting for a philosopher to concern himself with unjust suspicions, come, let us resolve your request as far as possible, and through you that of others, to whom indeed you promise the greatest things by this one manner of behavior. Ambitions, when one cuts away the roots that nourish the shoots of hymns, are indeed unappealing. For not even to Alexander, the son of Philip, did it remain free from envy and jealousy, seeing Philip parading himself with such frequent victories; but having become faint-hearted, he is said to have uttered a certain bacchic speech to his contemporaries, and, so to speak, one full of divine frenzy. For he said he was distressed that, since his father had forestalled everything, he would leave him no pretext for triumphs. I pass over most things in silence; but now, having once again brought forward a pretext, I wished, having bypassed this ambitious contention, to be myself the willing betrayer of opportunity and of my own fortune. For it is grave and wearisome that the task is one of no ignoble struggles, as you yourself know, and as you have proposed such a thing to certain reputable men; then, they in whom arrogance and a disorder of judgment tyrannize the mind out of season have all wisdom seated upon their eyebrows and make everything easy and light through the volubility of their tongue and their promises, proclaiming something highly ignoble. Nevertheless, since your grand tongue, reaping from every side what has been conceded for victory, both from a philosophical mind and from rhetorical persuasion, moves and brings forth other things to us for persuasion, and since it is not fitting for a wise and truth-loving man to concern himself with unjust suspicions, come, let us resolve your request to the extent possible, and through you that of others, to whom indeed you promise the greatest things by this one manner of behavior.
To all, indeed, who possess any share of intellect and critical science, it is acknowledged that the great Synesius did not train himself in a single kind of wisdom, but rather there is nothing which he was not; and I do not think there is anyone who would contradict this. For not only did he become a student of the entire breadth of Greek erudition to the highest degree, but he was also a participant in all the Chaldean rites; and he remained no stranger to those books and mystical initiations which the sacred scribes of the ancient Egyptians transmitted to later times, nor to those things which the theologians of Delphi dogmatized. But these things, and together with them, whatever Plato traveling from Athens and Pythagoras from Samos to the Egyptians gained—this man also energetically participated in such a feast. He even took care to know all that the voices of sorcerers and magicians instill; yet he never practiced these things. The act of bringing the words of these men, which lie in secret, to the ears of the learned—this he did, and this most of all. For it is not permitted for a man who is wise and a lover of learning to...
turn his eyes to this theater of the universe, which is in all respects adorned, without exploring and investigating the reasoning of all things and of the institutions, as far as it is possible to attain them, so that he may hold fast to some and abstain from others. And so long as he was a Greek by reputation, he held on to almost everything; but from the time he washed away that bitter hearing, he then abstained from most things, such as were a nuisance—like weeds—to the pure and well-ripened crop.
One might know this from the other books and discourses he has written; but one might know it even better from this present discourse, and learn as well of the man's great learning and discernment, and how his purpose is philosophical everywhere, and how a dignifying and solemn character blossoms upon his lips. But here most especially, since he is a fellow-beholder and a fellow-reveler in the inner mysteries of universal and supernatural investigations, and is as one inspired, just like the divine seers and those possessed by Phoebus. For, in order that I may use his own confessions regarding the state of his intellect here, he shows to the philosopher and teacher Hypatia that it was God who moved and compelled him to publish the present discourse, and he himself merely employed the pen, as if he had gone out of himself; but God wrought the whole thing with the utmost speed, providing abundant fountains of extemporaneous words, beyond even what he himself, as he says, encountered later on, in some parts using things that were most difficult and not at all resembling those that were alien to his own work.
And this is clear from what follows. For since the thought of the present discourse is guarded and fortified by lofty and ethereal divine inspirations, and thus discourses upon theology, it was necessary to draw a calmness from the diction, and not to confuse the underlying meaning with the strangeness of its usage, nor to cheat the eagerness of ears fond of learning. Yet the diction did not remain uninspired, nor did it take on a weightiness from a mind so inflamed, so as to soothe the discourse with a smiling tongue; rather, it used the familiar style here as well. For nature, having bestowed upon his tongue the facility and grace as if from a self-flowing fountain, he, by using some strange and, in some places, foreign customs and words, strikes the ear, and as it were stings it and pricks it, just as the thorn does to the palm of those harvesting the rose. For it is the way of men who live in different places that a certain idiosyncrasy of character and language follows them like a natural affection, and it happens that from there, if a writing arrives at an Attic-favoring ear, it brings with it a sea of some such character. For in Ptolemais and Alexandria, and in the region superior to Libya, dwelling far away, although they are carried by the same things, but as...
each may happen to have an opinion or understanding—some come bringing an unadulterated judgment, others one that is altogether wretched and corrupting the essential points, and others one that is half-wretched and leaning to both sides. There is fear, too, lest any of those who are in such a state, or in that, might speak amiss and smear upon us a charge of an opinion that is not good; and then, having partaken of a tongue [and diction] more common and prone to malice, might put us to the test, and, though we have done no wrong, we might receive an ill prize and one hostile to this noble pursuit. Nevertheless, we, like those who translate the opinion and language of the Indians and Persians into our own hearing and voice, remain blameless; in the same way, we too, clarifying an alien opinion and conveying it to a clearer hearing, shall certainly remain blameless before all those who are among the learned, and whose minds are not the least besieged and agitated by envy. And then, there will always be more, I suppose, of those who do not envy, to whom this matter will appear necessary, for whose part, while they eagerly join in wishing us the best, may God give us some reward for the work; but as for the envious, may they perish miserably by their own malicious envies.
it was as a stinging gadfly or a thorn. For among men who inhabit different places, a peculiar change of some custom at once and of language is wont to attend, just as a certain ingrained affection of the soul, and to arise thence. And if any commentary should arrive thence to an ear refined by Attic speech, it attracts to itself and assumes a sea of some such kind of discourse. For, staying in Ptolemais and Alexandria and in the upper part of Libya, he had from a remote region habit with those who were members and disciples of the Stoic and Academic families. Yet, living not by their laws, he was their companion; and because of this, he admitted thence some opinions, as it were, while he repudiated others. In this manner, he emulated these modes of speaking that originated from there, but others not at all. And therefore, using a certain august and imperial license of nature, he emigrated from his ancestral hearths, having little regard for those whose common conversation he enjoyed. Therefore, if anyone should posit this as the cause—and that his present discourse is, for the most part, difficult to conjecture, and as it were enigmatic and obscure—he does not run or wander far from what is decent and consistent. For a mind so burdened, and a collection of every discipline and wisdom, woven together with such exuberant eloquence, would bring much night and darkness, if anyone should wish to curiously investigate the internal and companionable sense. As if it were permissible for anyone, as a necessity, to explain this discourse according to the rationale of the intention and his own plan: that not only he who had been a participant before should become capable of all the things he discusses, as if he had begged for them, but also that he should be able to transform himself towards all kinds of opinion, and mostly of our own disparate ones, and, as it were, to change color towards varying hues, like that Pharian Proteus. For not all do approach from the same opinion, but as each has been allotted an opinion or insight: these indeed approach, bringing an integral and sincere judgment; others, however, a very disgraceful one, inasmuch as it errs with insane zeal regarding things that are consistent and appropriate; some, furthermore, one that is semi-vicious and leaning in both directions. And let no one of those who are affected in this or that way, through fear, reply perversely and incoherently, and sprinkle us with the crime and atrocious injury of a wicked opinion, and then, arrogating to themselves much of the tongue, the ignoble, the petulant, and those prone to malice, burden us with sinistral swelling before the multitude; and we, stained by no crime, bear away an ill-fated and hostile reward for this honest and excellent study. Nevertheless, with those who transfer the opinion of the Indians and Persians to our hearing and discourse, they remain blameless: in this way, even we, glowing with foreign glory and transferring it to a more lucid hearing, shall remain altogether blameless before those who are of the number of the sensible and elegant men, and whose mind envy, sitting over them, in no way shakes or disturbs. Afterwards also, there will be, I suppose, always more who will emulate this, to whom this matter will seem very useful. For whom, certainly, with favorable and eager spirit we wish the best, for us indeed may God give some reward of this work: but to the detractors, may they perish miserably with the very malicious detractions themselves.
This explanation of ours will not be made solely for the sake of the more perfect, but now also for the sake of those who are less perfect in their understanding. For some will apply themselves to this part, others to that, and some to both, according to the capacity and will of each. As for what he discusses regarding the soul, and with which of the ancient sages he speaks in agreement, and with which he disagrees—and what he says the imagination is, and what each of those others says—and where in the body he sets the tripod for the divination that occurs in sleep, he indicates this himself to the student of wisdom and teacher Hypatia, because many things that had not yet been philosophized upon by the Greeks had been brought forward. It is clear, therefore, that we also shall make our explanation according to her; for we shall mark each thing, being present at the place of each. But you, for the things that are well and rightly constituted according to your own mind, do not be sluggish in returning thanks in your heart and tongue to God; but for those things that are otherwise, provide us with a manifest expostulation. For of the former he is the guide, leader, and chorus master, but in the latter we are to be blamed.
INCIPIT EXPLANATIO
PAG. 130. *If indeed there are dreams.* Just as the precious treasures of kings, and whatever is sacred and honorable among men, are covered by different veils one after another, so that they may not lie exposed to unholy crowds to be debased, nor be held in contempt—for because of their price, that which is easy and ready at hand is cheapened—so too are those things which are seen by those who are sleeping. Since, as he says, a vision is more divine and prognostic, the imaginative spirit of the soul becoming at that time purer, because it has been set free in some way from bodily and material and gross tumults, these things also are given to men in an enigmatic way, as if under a veil. He says that their being wise and not clear is something of this kind; and he confirms this also through the verses of Hesiod. For he says: "The gods hid the effortless life from men, and before all good things the gods placed sweat." He says that to obtain good things without labor is a divine gift. And that this is given not to very many, but to very few: as wisdom without labors was awarded to Solomon; and to Samson, the insurmountable strength and vigor of the body; and again, as the liberal disciplines of the Greeks teach and urge, a certain wisdom not lowly in origin came from the providence above both to Aesop the Phrygian, and before that...
to Hesiod, and indeed to Homer. Since this wise man, Synesius, considers the notion that dreams are prophets, and that the sights seen during sleep are shadowy images of things to come, to be so certain and irrefutable that he wishes, right from the proem, to use certain demonstrative syllogisms; but because he does not have many followers in this, he restrains the boldness of his venture with a conjunctive and, as it were, distributive bond, saying, as it were: "If you agree with me in these dogmas, whoever you are who encounters this commentary, and admit that dreams are prophets and interpreters of oracles, and that things seen while sleeping offer foreknowledge of the future, you must also know as a necessary consequence that dreams—or at least the wise ones—are not exactly clear and open." And from this he concludes that what is least conspicuous and obvious is something ingenious and wise, contradicting Euripides. For he, in the play of Orestes, brings forward Menelaus saying: "What is clever and sharp is clear and perspicuous, not that which is obscure." Therefore, he formed and wove the proem according to the argument we have mentioned into the form of a hypothetical syllogism.
Life. He calls it free from labor here.
Greatest. Benefits.
With the virtue of knowing. That is, with the rational power of the soul.
To know. For it does not need this from without, but is itself knowledge.
To man. An aid to foresight.
For the many. The common crowd.
What is present. What now exists.
Of what is not yet. Of the future.
Having happened. With ambiguity.
Of the Panhellenes. Of all the Greeks, as many as campaigned against Troy.
The things before. That which has passed.
And Homer. For he says that Zeus is also prior in generation.
To contribute. To run together.
Since. For he said above that in this God differs from man, and man from the beast. C 
Verses. Homeric ones, and these too.
Rudely. That is, privately, and crassly, and ignorantly.
According to it. Poetry.
Philosophy. He spoke this aptly. For since the poets were philosophers, it escaped notice that they were called poets by everyone. For, acting similarly to physicians, they deposited their own philosophy into myths, verses, and soul-charming melody, and in this way covered up the bitterness of the benefit derived from them, just as the sons of physicians often hide the unpleasantness of beneficial medicines by tempering them with honey; so that, consequently, it is comprehensible to the wise, while to the profane it is pleasant to the people, but incomprehensible. For they interpret "Zeus" as nothing else than the most ancient and primordial Mind, and hence the most wise; just as they interpret "Athena" as wisdom, which they also say was born from the head of Zeus. Consistently, therefore, this wise man Synesius here interprets Zeus as Mind, and his strength as wisdom; and "prior in generation" as referring to the antiquity from which wisdom, for the most part, comes to those who possess it. Anaxagoras also says that Mind is what orders all things and is the cause of all things, so that it is shown from this that he too considers the Mind to be older than this universe. Than minds. Meaning intellectual substances. In this way. Thus, in such a manner as this. He fasteneth. In this way is our Synesius here a follower of the doctrines of Plato, and he so hangs upon his language that he has not only attained, by meditation, to the peak and sublimity of most of his opinions, as we shall show as we proceed, but he also uses his very expressions in many places. And this "he fasteneth" he also borrowed from Plato. For he says in the *Phaedo* that every pleasure and pain, as if having a nail, fastens the soul to the body, and attaches it, and makes it corporeal, believing those things to be true which the body would declare. He fasteneth. That is, he receives it into his own right, and cements it. With the strength. That is, because he is superior in strength to the others. He turns back. He is gathered, he ascends, as if concluding in a circle. Because. That is, for the reason that. These indeed. He said these are proofs—namely, that knowledge and wisdom are able to foresee future events, and that the art of divination is occupied with these things. But if it means. Since he is a Greek in his opinion, he relates the things that they themselves believe; and he says that the world is endued with soul, and then—Just as, he says, each of the animals in the world is composed of parts and members, which indeed have fellowship and kinship with one another, so also the world, consisting of heaven and earth and the things between them, being a living creature endued with soul,
experiences and performs like things toward every living creature. And its parts, having fellowship and brotherly concord with one another, signify and portend all things about to happen in mutual concord, some more, some less. Proceeding thereafter, he compares the world to a book, and all its parts—I mean animals and plants, and air and water, and stones, and all other things—to letters. Just as, therefore, the letters in books belonging to different languages are read—some by the sages of the Egyptians, others by those of the Greeks; and some read less and still imperfectly, just as infants do, either by syllables, or collectively, or the word all at once, while others read the discourse at once. For a discourse (logos) is a whole proposition, consisting summarily of a noun and a verb, for example, "Socrates walks." For it declares an intention, either true or false. A term (lexis) is a part of the discourse, for example, "Socrates," which declares neither truth nor falsehood. A syllable is a part of a term, for example, from "Socrates," only the "So," which signifies nothing. Thus, therefore, he says, the wise prophets and augurs also, reading the things that are in the world, foresee the future: some from the stars, or from those that remain in the fixed sphere, or from the torches that dart about in the air, which are not properly stars, but nevertheless appear to be stars, and he calls them here by the common name; but concerning the sun, and the moon, and the planets, and furthermore he had not spoken of comets, because the wise see the future from them, either as being admitted by all that they reveal it, or because he was content to have mentioned the neighboring things, and thought it obvious from these that those also are understood. Some, therefore, he says, foresee the future from the stars; others from the entrails of the sacrificed animals, which is indeed called sacrificial and dissectional divination. Augurs did not use all the entrails for the manifestation of future things, but the liver alone; and the entrails are seven: heart, liver, spleen, stomach, lungs, and two kidneys.
Portend. Manifest, they deliver.
Letters. Heavenly, aerial, terrestrial, marine.
By nature. One who has read through the nature of beings by experience.
Another. Wise.
The torches. These appear in the elemental fire, where there is, as it were, an interspace of air and ether.
In the clangor of birds. These are specifically the theories of the ornithoscopists; but others foresee the future through symbols and marks, which they themselves noted by experience; I mean the twitching of eyes or the heart, or of some other part and limb, or the tingling of the nose, or the meeting of some
inauspicious thing on the road, which common people call an evil "encounter," or some word spoken by chance; just as it once happened to Paullus Aemilius, the Roman general sent against the opposing army of Perseus, king of the Macedonians. For it is said that as he was about to depart, he found his little daughter in tears as he was embracing her, and having heard from her the cause of her grief, she said, "Perseus is dead." Now this Perseus, of whom she spoke, was a little dog that had been raised with his daughter. He answered, "With good fortune, my daughter, I accept the omen." The eloquent and learned Cicero sets forth these things in his book *On Divination*.
"Portents": so called by many.
"Events": occurrences, encounters, which happen for one reason, but result in something else.
"Of all things, meaningful": cohering by a certain mutual consent.
Accordingly, if there were wisdom! Wishing to show even more clearly the community and kinship that the parts of the world have with one another, he says that if wisdom were in birds just as it is in humans, birds would have established an art from humans, just as humans have crafted from birds the art of divination, which they call augury or bird-watching. For just as birds have always existed from the beginning, from the moment this whole universe was composed until now, so too humans have always existed; and both are, to both of them, ancient and new. Therefore, just as birds are suitable and apt for divination for humans, so too humans would have been suitable and apt for divination for the birds, in reverse, if wisdom were also in the birds. For it was necessary that the parts of the world participate with one another, since they are members of one living being, and therefore have sympathy for one another, and, so to speak, a "conbreathing." Saying these things, he as it were recovers himself, and is perplexed, and seems to ask himself, saying: "Might these be the *iunges* of the magicians?" For the wise man who knows this mutual kinship of these things, seeing that they are charmed by one another, and receive indication from one another—one thing dragging another, just as iron is dragged by a magnet—so too through this or that matter, and this figure, and this voice, this or that is elicited, as will be explained more clearly in what follows. For not only are trees charmed by trees, just as one palm is drawn by another, and herbs by herbs, and animals by animals, but even a certain fish is drawn by garlic, and other things by others; and what is more wonderful, demons from the air and earth are charmed by these stones, or by these herbs, or by these sounds, or by these figures, which they also call "characters." I believe these were discovered by the Chaldaeans and Egyptians from the beginning as a sign of recognition for each individual demon. They say that the *iunx* is a bird...
Regarding the names which they pronounce, they give a directive, saying: "Never alter the barbarian names." For there are names given by God to each [people], having an ineffable power in sacred rites, such as Sabaoth, Adonai, Cherubim, and Seraphim, Abraham, Isaac, and Jacob, and all such names; and he says that if you translate them into the Greek dialect, you erase their power and efficacy, as also seems to the wise Origen. Asclepius, the disciple of Hermes Trismegistus, says in his discourse to the Egyptian king: "As much as is in your power, O King, preserve the teaching handed down to you by us, so that such mysteries may not come to the Greeks, nor may the proud and embellished phraseology of the Greeks make the hallowed, solid, and efficacious power of the names grow feeble." For such words, when interpreted into the ancestral dialect, contain a clear understanding of the words—for the very quality of the voice and the power of the Egyptian names contain within themselves the efficacy of what is being said—but when they are distorted and converted into another language, they become obscure and ineffective.
Voces. Incantations and charms. And that of the digitus pedis [toe]. I myself once saw a boy who had been struck by an arrow in the tendon of his neck, and it happened that from that moment the other foot was seized by a kind of numbness, and the affliction remained incurable for the rest of his life; and while the other foot grew as he got older, this one remained in the thinness and shortness it had at that time, suspended, useless, and idle, like a lifeless weight. Therefore, it causes puzzlement to those who do not know the causes and reasons, how it was that the hand, being nearer, remained without any sensation of pain, but the foot, while lying far away, suffered with it. But as many as contemplate the things in the world with reasonable judgment, such people would find many similiar things. For not all bodies feel the same pain, nor do all the parts of the body, but only that body which is naturally prepared to suffer, and that part of the body which is apt to suffer with it, and at the time and in the manner it is natural to do so. Just as, when a sweet scent is spread through the air, neither wood, nor stone, nor indeed the ear, nor the hand, nor the foot perceives it, but only the sense of smell; just as also of melody, only the ear, and of colors, only the sight.
In inguen [groin]. The Greeks call the beginning of the thighs the groin, or as it were the upper beginning of the foot.
Unius enim ambo. Aristotle also writes in the book *History of Animals* that oxen feel less pain in their feet if someone anoints their horns with mud, or pitch, or oil.
Præstigiis decipitur. There is one thing that is *goetia*, and another that is *magia*, and another that is *pharmacia*. *Goetia* indeed is that which consists in invoking certain spirits of matter, the impure and the maleficent demons. The name is derived from *gooi* [griefs], as if it were causing things worthy of lamentation.
Magic is that power which operates through intermediate demons, both immaterial and material; pharmacy is that which is harmful through food and drink. Likewise, there is another category of *kakotechnia* (maleficent art), another of *pseudotechnia* (falsifying art), and another of *mataiotechnia* (futility). For *kakotechnia* is that which was invented by sorcerers, poisoners, and magicians to the detriment of mankind; *pseudotechnia* is that which shows things to be false, such as that which deceives the eyes; *mataiotechnia* is that which vanishes together with the air that has been struck.
*On the Hypate.* These things are taken as examples from the *Division of the Harmonic Canon*. We must, therefore, starting from there, unfold these matters for the sake of the clear understanding of those who happen upon them. The consonances through which harmonic systems are produced are many, the first of which is the *epitrits* [epitritic], which is called "through four" [the fourth]; it consists of two tones and a limma, which is also abusively called a semitone, in which the note of the fourth string, when compared to that of the first, is always found to have the epitritic ratio. The second consonance is the *hemiolios* [hemiolic], which is produced through five strings, that is to say, from three [tones] and a limma. Here, too, the note of the fifth string, when compared to the note of the first, is always found to have a hemiolic ratio. By the "first string" here, I mean the fourth of the first consonance, that "through four." For the fourth string of the two consonances is common; it is the end of the one "through four" and the beginning of the one "through five," that is, the limit of the *epitrits* and the beginning of the *hemiolios*. These two consonances, when combined in this way, produce the system called "through all" [the octave]. We must leave the other consonances for now, as they do not contribute much to the demonstration of the question before us. However, this system of the "through all" [octave] consists of eight strings, or notes, and five tones and two limmata. The note of the eighth string, when compared to the note of the first, is always found to have a double ratio.
The first string is called *nete*, the second *paranete*, the third *paramese*, the fifth *superparhypate*, the sixth *parhypate*, the seventh *hypate*, and the eighth the *proslambanomenos*. For it was added later by Pythagoras, as the ancient lyre of Orpheus was seven-stringed. Of such strings, some are acute in their notes, some are grave, and some are intermediate. The *hypatai* are grave, the *netai* are acute, and those between them are intermediate. But for greater clarity, we have laid out the eight-stringed rule grammatically, that is, the system of the "through all" [octave], which is composed of two tetrachords and one tone, or of two epitritic ratios and one *epogdoos* [superoctave] ratio, or rather of one epitritic and one hemiolic. For the *epogdoos* ratio, when linked to one epitritic, produces a ratio...
of its own; but the epitritic ratio is observed in two unequal numbers when the greater contains the whole of the lesser and a third part of the lesser, as in the case of four and three. The *epogdoos* [superoctave], when the greater number contains the whole of the lesser and an eighth part of the lesser, as in the case of 8 and 9. The hemiolic [sesquialter], when the greater number contains the whole of the lesser, and half of the lesser, as in the case of 3 and 2. With these things laid down, this wise Synesius says, "He who has plucked"—the term "plucked" (*psēlas*) refers to some melodic musical sound, from the verb *psallō*—"he who has plucked the *hypatē*," he says, "he has not moved the one next to it, that is to say, the *proslambanomenē*." For this has an *epogdoos* ratio to it, but rather the *mesē* [middle string] near it, in relation to which it also has an epitritic ratio. For by the affinity of such ratios, they sympathize with one another in an ineffable way, even when they are far apart, more so than those that are closer.
Pythagoras’s seven-stringed lyre.
[Diagram omitted: The contents of the diagram are presented in the grid below, translating the Greek labels on the schema provided in the scan]
[Grid translation of the diagram labels]:
Hemiolic (fifth).
Nētōn tetrachord.
Hypatōn tetrachord.
Epitritic.
Diatessaron (fourth).
Nētē, Paranētē, Paramēsē, Mēsē, Lichanos, Hypar-hypatē, Parhypatē, Hypatē, Proslambanomenē.
Semitone, Tone, Tone, Semitone, Tone, Tone, Semitone.
paten, or the lowermost string opposite the lowest; (this word *psēlas* means "he who plucks," a certain term for a musical concinnity, from the verb *psallō*, "I sing by plucking strings"). He says, therefore, that he who has plucked the *hypatē* has not moved the one which is next to it—namely, the *assumpta* [proslambanomenē]—for it has the same *epogdoos* ratio with respect to it; but [he has moved] the *mesē* that is closer, in comparison to which it also has the epitritic ratio. For by the affinity of such ratios, they consent among themselves latently, and those which are far distant do so more than those which are closer.
Of the prior. A Which they had before they were endowed with form, when the matter was still shapeless.
Simply. Compositely one; for it would be simply and absolutely one, a flux.
Of many. With synthesis.
Of the station. Of the very station, of the very battle itself.
Of dissonants. Insofar as the acute and the grave are called dissonant.
Of concords. Because, strictly, the sesquitertian, sesquialter, and double ratios are called concordant, since they agree with one another in mutual harmony.
Harmony. It is.
Of a lyre. Upon a lyre.
Cosmic. Upon the cosmos.
Archimedes. This marvelous Archimedes flourished in Sicily in the time of the ruler Hieron. Having turned geometric science from the sphere of the intelligible to the corporeal, and having mixed mathematical reason with mechanical constructions through the benefit of sensory perception, he set it forth more effectively for the majority. So much so that when Marcellus, the general of the Romans, sailed against Syracuse (a great city of all Sicily) with many ships and engines, Archimedes resisted those ships and engines with his own artificial ones, and delivered the Roman fleet to such slaughter that the great Marcellus, having been defeated and momentarily turning back, greatly admired the man's wisdom and was amazed at his skill in such arts, and considered his strength to be much greater than that of the mythical hundred-handed giants. Therefore, he used to say that by the force assigned to it, a given weight can be moved; and he added, in confirmation of his demonstration, that if he had another earth, he would move it, by moving from this one to that one.
Wise. The prophet.
To him. To the cosmos.
Of the cosmos. Here he calls the cosmos that which extends from the lunar sphere down to the earth. For he says that this is both composed of the four elements and possesses a nature subject to passion and change in one way and another, and further, even subject to sorcery; but he says that everything outside of the cosmos—that is, the place above the moon—is a simple, uncompounded, and unchangeable world, and passionless, and free from sorcery, and more intellectual, and turned away from all matter that is formed from the things here below.
Free from sorcery. Free from passion.
He does not care. This is taken from Homer. For in the first rhapsody of Homer, Agamemnon says to Achilles:
"I do not care about you,
Nor do I heed your anger..."
Relentless. Passionless.
But that which is subject to passion. That which in the soul relates to reason and the intellect is passionless; but that which relates to matter—
is entirely subject to passion. It is divided, that is, when you view it, into two parts: that which possesses an affinity for all things, and that which does not obey reason. For we call the one nutritive and natural, but the other pulsative and vital; yet neither of these knows how to obey reason. For they possess neither power of choice nor any power of judgment. In like manner, that which is obedient to reason is twofold. For we call one part the concupiscible, and the other the irascible. The concupiscible part is divided into pleasure and pain; for when it attains its goal, it produces pleasure; but when it misses it, pain. The irascible part is divided into three: into anger—which they call bile and wrath—and into indignation, and into resentment. Now, anger is an impulse of the spirit towards the beginning and motion of an action, or a spirit in operation; indignation and resentment, however, are an abiding and persistent spirit.
In Divinations. They say that divination among the Greeks is multiform and operates in diverse ways, just as will be briefly stated in the progress of this book. They call a "rite" the communication of the mysteries, as it perfects those who are being initiated. Furthermore, they call those who have attained to the habit of this tradition "perfected" and "perfect."
Dissident. All things in the world are compound and formed into species; if you view them as essences, you perceive something one, having kinship and affinity for itself in every respect. But if you see them as things formed into species, you see a multitude that is distinct and has discord with itself—insofar as it relates to the whole; or rather, when you view existing things by species, you see the one as distinct; but when you view them according to the nature of being itself, you see the one as gathered together.
By the Law. For civil administration indeed forbids these rites.
And Truly the Whole. "But we," he says, "as far as it was possible for us, have traversed generally the panegyric of all divination, and we have shown that a common token of the best divination is obscurity." For things that are observed clearly and openly would not be any kind of divination, nor would they be in need of any demonstration. But our present intent and goal is to dwell upon the study of this very "best" [divination], and to make a more accurate examination of it.
The Whole. Generally and universally.
Of It. Of divination.
Of those within. According to potentiality.
Has been praised. By us.
Best. In respect to the other divinations.
Separated. Specifically.
Character. Token.
Reprehension. Refutation. Demonstration. In full nature. Manifestly and exactly. This is venerable. Obscure.
From there. Herodotus, writing the Persian affairs and composing them with others of his historical books, relates these things concerning the Greeks: that when the Athenians had sent to the Delphic oracle, the Pythian priestess, whose name was Aristonice, responded first as follows:
"Wretched one, why do you sit? Flee to the ends of the earth,
Leaving behind the dwellings and the high peaks of the wheel-shaped city.
For neither the head nor the body remains firm,
Nor do the feet or hands or middle;
