For it crumbles down. For fire and keen Ares,
Pursuing in a Syrian chariot, shall destroy many towers.
And many temples of the gods shall I give to the rapacious fire."
And first these, but secondly to the suppliants the Pythia responded thus:
"Not even by using many entreaties can Pallas
Placate Olympian Zeus, though she supplicates with many words and deep counsel.
But to you I will speak this word again, bringing it near to adamant.
For while all other things are being captured, as many as the limit of Cecrops
Holds within, and the recesses of sacred Cithaeron,
Zeus grants to the Triton-born a wooden wall,
To remain alone unsacked; this shall preserve your children.
Do not wait for the cavalry and the infantry coming
In a great host from the continent with peace, but retire,
Turning your back; you shall yet meet them face-to-face.
O divine Salamis, you shall destroy the children of women,
Whether Demeter is sown or gathered."
In vain. Not understanding the mind. There. According to this. Concealment. The obscurity. It must be added. We must be diligent. Thus. By divination. The mind indeed. That which is closely joined to the Creator. The species. Of the first, second, third, and fourth.
Mind, soul. Existing, becoming.
Therefore, alternating. Wishing to show more clearly the proportion of those things proposed, or how mind and soul possess a ratio toward things that exist and things that become, he used definitions and phrases from the fifth book of Euclid’s elements. For Euclid says in the definitions of the fifth element, that an alternating ratio is the taking of the antecedent toward the antecedent, and the consequent toward the consequent; and let these numbers be suggested as an example: 16, 8, 4, 2. The ratio which 16 has toward 8, the same it has toward the 4 relative to the 2.
and 8 is the antecedent of 16. An alternating proportion, therefore, arises when you take the antecedent, 16, and compare it with the antecedent, 4, and the consequent, 8, with the consequent, 2. And it is shown again in the same way that whatever ratio 16 has to 4, the same ratio 8 has to 2. It shows, therefore, from this, that whatever ratio the mind has to the soul, the things that truly exist have the same to the things that come into being—and alternately, whatever ratio the mind has to things that exist, the soul has the same to things that come into being.
*Conversely.* This is also from the writings of Euclid. For he says in the definitions of the fifth book of the *Elements* that an inverse ratio is the taking of the consequent as an antecedent, compared to the antecedent as a consequent. But for the sake of clarity of the ratio, let 16 and 8 be taken from the proposed numbers. 16, then, is the antecedent, and 8 is the consequent; but if we take 8 as the antecedent and compare it to 16 as the consequent, the inverse ratio is made. He says, therefore, that here also, if we take the soul as the antecedent and compare it to the mind as the consequent, and likewise the things that come into being to the things that exist, the inverse ratio is made, and nonetheless it is found to be true also in this way, as an axiom proposed by us here concerning these things.
*Axiom.* Established dogma.
*The soul has.* As in this diagram:
[Diagram: Mind – Soul; Things that exist – Things that come into being]
*It puts forth.* The soul.
*It reflects. It illuminates, it teaches.
*Before they were in the common.* Reason, or as if through the interior word.
*Governing.* Or as the mind.
*Power.* The mind indeed communicates with the rational soul by means of the word; but the rational soul communicates with the sensitive soul, and with the organic senses, by means of the phantastic spirit; but again, the sensitive soul communicates with the natural through the nourishing and growth-giving spirit. Plotinus also says that just as the spoken word is an image of the word in the soul, so the soul is an image of the mind.
*To declare.* To the soul itself.
*To the soul.* To the rational one.
*Impressions.* Types.
*This life.* The phantastic and sensory life, by which we communicate with the irrational, or rather the activity of the phantastic spirit during sleep.
*And in property.* For it is seen specifically in rational animals.
*Organs of sense.* Obscure and more powerful, through which postera ratio, and nonetheless that which has been proposed by us concerning these things is found to be just as true, like an axiom and a pronouncement.
*Constitutum.* It is prescribed.
*Promit.* Namely, the soul.
*Et illuminat.* It illustrates and teaches.
*Priusquam eas communioni.* To reason, or at any rate through reason conceived by the mind.
*Vis animi moderatrix.* Or at any rate the mind.
*Vis.* The mind communicates with the rational soul through the intervening reason: but the rational soul communicates with the sensitive, and with the organic parts of sensing, through the medium and interposed phantastic spirit. Again, the sensitive soul communicates with the natural through the spirit of nourishing, vegetating, and growing. Plotinus further says that just as in speech oration is an effigy and likeness of the reason which is in the soul, so also the soul is an image of the mind.
*Manifestas fecerit.* To the soul itself.
*Primaria animi parte.* Namely, the rational.
*Imagines.* Types, forms, and likenesses.
*Vita hæc.* Phantastic, and as regards the faculty of sensing, the life through which we communicate with those lacking reason; or rather, the action of the phantastic spirit, which is vigorous during dreaming.
*In proprietate naturæ.* For it seems [to exist] separately in animals that use reason.
*Sensuum organa.* Obscure and more potent, through which
these things are made manifest and visible, and possess their efficacy. *For colors.* First through the phantastic spirit, then through the external senses, which like channels receive the powers proceeding thence in various ways, and [act] according as each is situated and placed, just as happens in the passage of the air. *And of touch.* For he said above that the mind has the forms of things that truly exist, but the soul has the form of things that happen and come to pass: and similarly also the phantasy, and even more so. *Of those lacking efficacy.* Because the soul, he says, anticipated the knowledge of things that happen, but being constrained and intertwined by carnal cohesion, it requires also bodily instruments, so that it may be taught by them and recall [them] to memory. *And if perhaps.* Or [it is] as [something] better than the organic senses. *This genus.* Or as the phantasy and its sense-organs. *According to it.* The phantastic sense-organ. *They admonish.* The gods. *They exercise.* They prophesy to the gods. *Providing.* For us. *If indeed.* For it is possible in dreams to be guided toward the discovery of a treasure. *Neither if anyone.* Lying down. *Afterward.* They say also that the poet Hesiod, being formerly a shepherd of sheep, having one day laid himself down to sleep beside Mount Cithaeron in Thessaly, thought he saw the Muses approaching him and nourishing him with a laurel leaf, and that immediately upon waking he became a wise and most skillful poet. *Poet.* As Hesiod. *He brought.* Hesiod and others. *Plots.* Also Hannibal the Carthaginian, having been defeated by Scipio the African, fled into Bithynia to Prusias; but not long after, Titus the commander, having traveled thither from Rome on account of some other necessity and business, and having seen Hannibal, and suspecting the cleverness and experience of the man, lest he might at some time return to Carthage and Libya, plotted secretly to slay him cruelly. Hannibal, having learned this through certain dreams, took himself out of the land of the living by taking poison, finally saying this: "Let us now at last put an end to the great anxiety of the Romans, who have considered it troublesome, difficult, and late to await the death of the hated old man, but desire, like a fledgling bird, maimed by age, to take him off in death cruelly." *Unharmed.* Without loss or damage. *Sickness.* That there are indeed very many others, it would not be unbelievable, but above all would be Aristides the orator; this is also shown by the sacred writings inscribed under his name.
*Instruction.* The ascent. 
*Let him hear.* Seek those things; for he is silent; for he does not state these things in their entirety. 
*The interior.* The cognitive power existing within. 
*Successive.* From its own, he sowed. 
*Strength.* Of power. 
*Good fortune.* Since it comes from us. 
*Dream.* From the dreams. 
*Of which he sowed.* He (the author) says that dreams come from God; but Aristotle says that dreams are not God-sent. For if they were, he says, God-sent, they would be sent only to those who live in accordance with virtue, and who are occupied with divine matters; but now we see that some wicked and ignorant men, knowing neither reason nor virtue, also see them. For it happens that some such persons are even more prone to true dreams than the good, by reason of the good temperament of their body. For these often keep the images and forms of the things seen in the phantastic faculty unconfused, some of which happen to be true. Aristotle, therefore, and many other secular philosophers, forbid that dreams be God-sent; but that there is a certain nature of spirits that has dominion over the things that appear in sleep, this he readily concedes.
*For the harvest.* Wishing to show that attaining [knowledge] is not less than learning, he says from abundance that it is even more, so that he may attain that which is equal. 
*Phantasia.* In some books it is written 'life' (accusative), in others 'of life' (genitive). For it was necessary that both be set down; but because of the awkwardness, the 'one' was passed over in silence. Yet the 'one' is posited from both, as one might wish; and it takes the other from without, elliptically. 
*Towards those.* They are not even deemed worthy of the lesser good things. 
*To know.* Those who merely seem wise. 
*Disowned.* Of the divine Egyptian precepts. 
*Not of sacrifices.* That is, as if there were no need. 
*These toys.* For they are trifles. 
*Those who.* Those who prophesy through sacrifices and entrails. 
*Arts indeed.* He calls these what he earlier termed the 'iynges' of magicians, having called those who deign to practice these things 'magicians'; and he calls the various arts 'iynges', whatever were performed by them in a manifold way through sacrifices and the cutting of entrails. 
*What then.* What then, he says, is absurd, that a wise man, in that he is wise, should exist even more and beyond the commonality, and in this way become wise?
*Thereby wise.* And thus, or as even through those of the navel boundary they made it dwell, where God [is] the sleeper. *Therefore.* For indeed. *More divine.* More godlike. *More public.* More common. *Blessed.* But also impossible. *Through phantasy.* For through phantasy alone it would be near to being able to see God of itself, as far as is attainable. *Elder.* Therefore, more honorable. *Sense.* As ruling over the organic senses. *Most common.* As being participated in by the various sensory organs. *And body.* This also is a body, he says, as being more akin to the body, and mediating between soul and body; and he called it the first body, as speaking immediately to the soul. For the corporeal sensory organs do not speak immediately to the soul, but through the medium of the phantastic [faculty].
*But that.* The phantastic spirit dwells in the head of man.
*Of the animal.* And these things, then, [are] his. But Plato in the *Timaeus* says this: "But indeed the desire for food and drink of the soul, and whatever it holds of need because of the nature of the body, they settled in the region between the midriff and the navel: where God, he says, constituted the form of the liver, and placed for its dwelling that which is full of juice and plump, polished, bright, and sweet, and having bitterness he fashioned it, so that in it the power carried down from the intellect—like [as] in a mirror receiving types, and providing for the seeing of images—might, when using a part of the bitterness, cause this and that; but when using the sweetness by nature conjoined with it, and directing all things in it straight, smooth, unimpeded and free, it might make the portion of the soul that dwells about the liver gracious and cheerful, and having a moderate way of life in the night, using divination in sleep, since it had no part in reason and intelligence. Wherefore they established in it the oracle. And it is a sufficient sign that God has given the art of divination to human folly. For no one of sound mind attains to divination [that is] divine and true, but rather one who, in sleep, is shackled in the power of [his] intelligence, or through disease, or by some enthusiasm is altered." And a little later he says, that "the nature of the liver, for these reasons, is of such a kind and exists in the place that we say, for the sake of divination."
*But of sense.* Of the first. *By which.* Of the senses. *Sense.* Uniform.
Dispenses. Similarly, just as with air drawn through a flute; for while it is uniform, it is varied as it exits through the holes in the flute, becoming sharper, or deeper, or intermediate, or consonant, or dissonant.
And as they are. Speaking of the center and straight lines, he simultaneously makes mention in power and potentiality of a circle or a sphere. For it is impossible for a center to be conceived of and thought of without a circle or a sphere, just as a circle or a sphere [cannot exist] without a center. The geometric definitions therefore assert that a circle is a flat figure contained by one line, which is called the circumference, to which the straight lines falling from the center are equal to one another. A Here, therefore, he likens the imagination to a center and a root; and to the straight branches, the powers emitted from there as far as the organic senses—I mean, sight, and hearing, and the other three—all of which he declares to be indeed one when considered in relation to the center and the root, but many in accordance with their progression and spreading out from there.
He calls the imagination the common sense, and properly a sense and more divine, as it directly converses with the intellectual soul; but he calls that sense most animalistic, and crasser, and abusively termed "sense," which acts through the put-forward and projected, and visible, sensory organs—I mean, the eyes, and ears, and nose, and the rest.
*Most animalistic.* Through the superlative he indicated that it is crasser. 
*Sense.* For it is thickened, extending into the density of things in generation and corruption. 
*More divine.* Which he properly called the phantastic spirit. 
*The immediate sense.* Which he called "first" above; now it is called immediate sense, that is, imagination. 
*But if [through] the bodily senses.* That is, the organs of perception. 
*We have seen.* Namely, and what we have heard. 
*But it deceives.* It deceives, he says, for these three reasons: the eye. First, because of the nature of the things seen, just as a peacock's feather shows distinct colors according to the particular position. Second, because of the air and the water, and the interval. For the air is sometimes humid and thick, and sometimes dry and thin; and the interval of the thing seen is sometimes short, and sometimes long. And third, because of its own inability. 
*It deceives also.* For he says that the eye itself also deceives, lies, and deceives. 
*And by that which is perceived.* Namely, through the air and the intervening space.
The imaginative faculty. For because of the iniquity of the resolve, man draws his soul down into matter, and from there he makes the imaginative faculty susceptible to illness, as they say happens to many after food, and especially to children. For great is the exhalation from the food, and the motion is confused and uneven and difficult to distinguish, to which it comes about that either dreams do not occur at all, or they occur uneven and unlike and difficult to interpret; just as happens in water. For when it is calm and clear, the images appear distinct; but when it is moved and disturbed, either images are not seen at all, or they are unlike and difficult to interpret.
*By which.* Philosophy.
*Being purified.* Among the Greeks, he says, imagination is purified through initiatory rites; but the people of our pious Church purify it through temperance and vigilance and fasting. Thus, when the sensory power is purified, it becomes receptive of God; nevertheless, before calling and bringing God into itself, it first thrusts out the preceding sinful insinuations, as many as are introduced and enter into the imagination through the organic sensory organs. And this he says is a natural life: to keep the imagination pure from such things. For it is common to soul and body, and it must hold a pure communion with the soul.
*Those also which.* So that from this, those insinuations produced through the organic sensory organs also run out and withdraw, before God descends into the imaginative faculty as far as is possible. For such insinuations hasten to run out before God is invited in by the imaginative faculty; and they run out through spiritual purification.
*According to that which is natural.* Of the continent and temperate and virtuous man.
*Again, to be.* For not only of the body, but also of the rational soul and the mind.
*This.* The imaginative faculty.
*Not unsympathetic.* It is not alien to the nature of the soul, but it also runs together and suffers along with it, and does not remain by itself, having a peculiar nature like the body, which he also calls an "oyster-like covering" on account of the hardness and the resistance which it has toward the soul.
P. 137. *Oyster-like.* From many places it is shown that this wise Synesius is an ardent follower of Plato. For not only from the opinion—that he holds the same views as he—but also from the concepts, and from the methods, and from the very words themselves. For the saying "oyster-like covering" for the body has its origin from there. For he says also in the *Phaedrus*, [index: entries preserved]
regarding the initiation and the contemplation of those who are pure, being pure in a pure light, and having no mark of this which we now call the body, to which we are bound like an oyster.
And "being improved," and "being depraved," also have their origin from there. For Plato speaks of sin in this way: "The figures of the gods, being evenly balanced and easily guided, travel readily; the others with difficulty." For the horse burdened by partaking of vice, inclining and weighing down toward the earth, because it is not well nurtured by the charioteer; where indeed labor and ultimate struggle are proposed for the soul, and the rest.
*Being improved.* That is, the phantastic spirit for the soul pursuing a good way of life.
*Being depraved.* By matter and by stumbling.
*This.* The phantastic.
*Common terminus.* He calls the phantastic faculty of the soul a common terminus of both extremes, that is, of the nature of body and of the incorporeal, of reason and of irrationality, of matter and of privation, and by this middle point the opposites meet and are joined together. He calls things "divine" those that are immaterial and incorporeal; and "lowest" those that are material, corporeal, and sensible. For this reason he says it is difficult to define its nature and to declare what it is, just as we declare about the soul that it is an incorporeal and immaterial substance and beyond sense; and again about the body, that it is a material substance, flowing and composite.
For the phantasy is neither body nor entirely without body; and for this reason, it can admit neither the definition of body nor that of the incorporeal. For it gathers and begs something from both opposites—from the body, I say, and from the incorporeal—as much as is appropriate and fits its own nature, and a certain single nature is formed that is composed of natures so far removed from one another.
*Of both.* Of the rational soul and of the body.
*Divine things.* Of the phantastic.
*To be defined [to be designated].* To be grasped, contained, bounded, and understood.
*Of the phantastic essence.* That is, the phantasy itself.
*But it itself.* The nutritive, the vegetative, the sensitive.
*Of the beast.* That which is not at all composed of reason.
*It is purified.* Just as the spider, the bee, and the ant, which naturally perform works such as not even the most prudent of men could perform. But the young of monkeys, and those of bears, and of very many other animals, having a nature flexible for learning, are taught by men and become suitable for many human tasks.
*At least also.* Either naturally or through human training.
*Something more excellent.* Higher in knowledge.
*All of the demons.* The ancients, the Greeks, of the gods and of the demons...
stated that there was a difference, and they called the greater ones gods, and the lesser ones demons. Synesius, however, sometimes assigns a distinction between them, and at other times uses the names indiscriminately. *All the kinds of demons.* But, he says, all the kinds of demons that dwell in this world have obtained this life, which is to say, a sensory one. For in general, those kinds are nothing but phantasy. *In general.* According to their essence. *And eidolic.* Eidola, he says, are things that are bodyless, yet they have a certain thickness, some more, some less. It seems, therefore, that Synesius is here following the opinion of Democritus and Sextus and other Greeks. For they say that there are certain demons around the air and the earth, having a thickness and a certain cohesion and affection, and that they are invisible to healthy human eyes—which they also call "eidola" because they preserve, I believe, a certain imprint and shadowy tracing and image of the angelic and more immaterial nature from which they have fallen. They are difficult to destroy, but not immortal for all time; and some of them are harmful, while others are beneficial. They say, therefore, that these approach human beings, and especially when they are sleeping, and foretell many things that are to come; yet such predictions, when made by the good ones, are clear and stir up eagerness, but when made by the harmful and baleful ones, the opposite is true.
But the human phantasy is not a circle, but by a hidden power receives the shapes of things flowing in from without and is subject to them, just like a book being read and studied by the intellect; and in the case of clear and evident things, it teaches itself of its own accord what ought to be accepted; but in the case of obscure things, it also calls upon the intellectual powers of the soul, and becomes, as it were, a council chamber concerning things to be done. For the intellect, unless it receives its thoughts from the phantasy, has no activity concerning the generated and mutable nature of things. *Itself.* Or as a power. *With another.* Or as one with the intellect, for the most part. *Not without phantasy.* But intertwined with phantasy. *If, then, anyone.* Except if someone. *In an instant.* Just as the divine prophets and the divine Paul, having fallen into ecstasy. For then the intellect, having in a certain way gone outside of matter and having transcended the body, sees in an ineffable manner according to the nature of the intellect, without error. For this reason the divine Paul also says, "I know a man, whether in the body I do not know, or outside of the body I do not know,"* and so on. *In an instant.* In a momentary and indivisible and very brief interval.
Scholia on Synesius "On Dreams"
*There, a transition.* Look for the discourse of Plato which he entitles *Phaedo*. 
*It had of a kind.* Either as a divine illumination. *To look beyond.* Either as the intellect transcending the imagination. 
*And philosophy.* For Plato says in the discourse concerning the soul, from the person of Socrates: *It is difficult.* For as much as it is difficult, by so much is it blessed to look beyond. 
*He says.* Plato. 
*Devoid of imagination.* The intellectual. 
*Being projected.* Being set before and appearing. 
*The spirit, at any rate.* He calls the imagination sometimes the phantastic spirit, sometimes the animal spirit, sometimes the primary body of the soul, sometimes the spiritual soul, and sometimes the common boundary of the soul and the body. 
*Animal.* Either as the phantastic. 
*The blessed ones.* The sacred scribes of the Egyptians. 
*And a genius.* According to his own habit. 
*Penalties.* Punishments. 
*In this.* It repays. 
*And oracles.* Ancient. 
*By dream fantasies.* For such as is the life of each, such also are the fantasies in sleep, and they follow him according to probability, being, as it were, the echoes of daily cares; and such as the fantasies in sleep are, such it is likely for the soul to find the condition to be there after the departure from the body. *For there is great hope for one arriving there, where I am setting forth, that I may sufficiently, if anywhere else, attain there that for which much effort has been made by us in the present life; so that the journey now enjoined upon me happens with good hope, and for any other man also it is considered to prepare the intellect as if cleansed.* And a cleansing of it is to separate the soul as much as possible from the body, and to accustom it to gather and collect itself from everywhere out of the body, as if from bonds. 
*And philosophy.* That of Plato, and those who are in agreement with him. 
*To be of the second.* Of those after death. 
*The first ones.* Concerning which the imagination is occupied here. 
*It itself.* The phantastic faculty. 
*Being wiped away.* And therefore of the worse habit. 
*Stain.* Casting filth into it. 
*Is taken away.* The phantastic faculty. 
*Heat.* Which are brought about through fasting, and wakefulness, and humility.
And this therefore. The powers about the body of the soul, being many, communicate with the Divine only in the intellectual and reasoning faculty, which Plato with good reason called a wing, as it draws the soul up from mortal and earthly things toward heavenly and more divine ones, and does not allow its phantastic faculty to be stained by material affections. And this therefore. This is the purification of the phantastic faculty. *Caverns.* The depths. *Of the earth.* It puts on. Since this wise man Synesius, as we have said, is in all things a most keen follower of Plato, and here too uses many of his borrowed sentiments, it would be just to set forth his words in this place instead of any other interpretation. He says, therefore, in the *Phaedo* concerning the polluted and impure soul, that it is weighed down by that which is corporeal, which the very intercourse and communion with the body, because of their constant association and assiduous practice, has made innate and twofold. And one must consider this to be ponderous, heavy, earthly, and visible; and being encumbered by it, such a soul is weighed down and dragged back to the visible place by fear of the invisible and Hades, just as it is said to roll about monuments and tombs, around which indeed some shadowy phantasms of souls have been seen, of such a sort as these souls provide, being ghosts paying the penalty for an evil life previously led; and they wander about until, by the desire of the corporeal nature that accompanies them, they are again bound into a body. And they are clothed, as is likely, in such characters as they happened to have practiced in their life; as, for instance, it is likely that those who have practiced gluttony and wantonness and revelry put on the kinds of donkeys and similar beasts; those, however, who have preferred injustice and tyrannies and robberies put on the kinds of wolves and hawks and kites; but those who have practiced the political and popular virtue, which they call temperance and justice, having become so out of habit and practice, without philosophy and intellect, it is likely that they return again into such a political and gentle kind, or perhaps into bees, or wasps, or ants, or also into the human kind again, and it is likely that men of moderation arise from them; but to the kind of the gods it is not permitted to arrive for one who has not philosophized and who departs completely pure, except for the lover of learning; and he calls the philosopher a lover of learning, as one who is inquisitive and a searcher into the nature of things that are. *Wretched.* Unfortunate. *By toil.* And by punishments through time. *Purified.* The phantastic faculty.
*For having come to be.* The phantastic soul, as it came into existence from the beginning. 
*With the worse.* With the material things. 
*With the better.* With the intellectual things. 
*From above.* From the spheres, as he said before.
*And this.* And this, he says, is that the rational soul is able to release the phantastic spirit, which is heavily bogged down below, and to ascend—freed from the material relationship—toward the heights; this would be a gift of God, being supported by ineffable means and by a *telete*, that is, a mystical and diviner teaching that leads and disciplines it. Yet this does not happen to many souls, but with difficulty to one, or perhaps a second; nonetheless, he even complains about the ascent of this one. For he called it "wretched" and "disgraced," as having appeared unjust on account of not returning that which is foreign, from which it had borrowed.
*Nature.* That is, this is natural.
*Soul.* Rational.
*To flow together.* To follow voluntarily.
*To draw.* Unwilling.
*To be drawn.* Dragged.
*By wickedness.* By the material attachment.
*Having granted.* Having yielded.
*And this is.* For the Chaldean Oracles exhort us to stretch our mind upward continually toward God, and not to yield, allowing it to incline downward toward the terrestrial world, where nothing is trustworthy or firm, but all things are vain and false. One must consider how he called this terrestrial world, which he has also called "shrouded in darkness," "black-shining" (or dark-gleaming); and not only this, but also Hades. C D
A. He calls it so because the Chaldeans name Hades in many ways. For they consider it a god, and the leader of the extreme and terrestrial lot. There are times when they say the world under the moon is "shrouded in darkness," and they call this Hades; and at other times the lunar sphere itself, which they also establish as the border between the ethereal world and the material one. They call "the Abyss" the matter which they place in the region under the moon. 
*Terrify.* The sacred oracles of the Chaldeans terrify.
*Rationis capax (Ratiocinative).* He speaks of the soul, as having its existence from God.
*Infidus (Faithless).* Unstable.
*Amphiknephēs (Shrouded in darkness).* Dark.
*Rypōn eidōlocharēs (Filthy and idol-loving).* Delighting in idols, where gods dwell, and where the more material demons frequent; and, he says, the phantastic spirit becomes thickened.
*Emplēktos (Stolid).* As if struck by lightning.
*Tō de eidōlō (To the idol).* Here, too, by "idol" he means the faculty of phantasy, which, being a psychic spirit and subtle, nevertheless, because of the wickedness of its choice, sometimes has a certain thickness—such or such—which he calls a condition of the spirit; and then it truly becomes an idol itself, that is, similar to those idolatrous demons.
*Hē katō (The one below).* He had called it the black-shining world earlier.
*Homoio gar (For to the like).* For since the phantastic spirit, instead of bearing the image of the soul, and being a likeness of it, became rather, through deviation, an idol similar to material demons, it naturally also finds pleasure in their companionship.
*Ei de en (But if it is one).* But if the intellect also is led down with the phantasy, and they become one single thing out of both.
*Kai ho nous (And the intellect).* Perhaps the intellect, having become more "idol-like" and thicker, might also accept (or welcome) this finding of pleasure.
*Kaito touto kakōn (And yet this is the greatest of evils).* He says, not to perceive an evil that is present, but to find pleasure in it as if it were a good thing, would be the greatest of evils. For to find pleasure in such things and not to perceive them is the mark of those who do not even look up or seek to receive correction.
*He cites as an example the scirrhus; for a scirrhus is a dirt that is difficult to wash away and hard to cleanse. 
However, the statement seems obscure to many, because Synesius, using brevity, did not expand upon such an example, but wove it into the text. It would have been clearer had he said the following: that just as a scirrhus, by entering into the depth of some underlying body, becomes difficult to wash away and hard to purge and hard to cleanse; so too does the preoccupation with matter, having submerged the intellect, and—if I may say—having possessed it in its depths, and having made it as it were thicker, steals its assent through pleasure, and the earth, and D [it fails] to take remembrance or ...
it does not permit the recollection of salvation and the thought of safety and liberation to be taken from that vehement commotion. Yet, a remedy is found that is linked to higher meditation, which is salutary repentance; for repentance is the change of counsel of a man who is bearing things heavily and with an ill spirit, and who is shuddering throughout his whole body at the depravity occupying him. For to pursue vice with hatred has its beginning in the will and free choice: for the will is the root and foundation of all words and deeds, and to it they extend and stretch the causes and the first principles. For unless the will concurs, every instruction and learning remains lifeless and ineffective, and incomplete. For if the teacher teaches, but the will of the student is unwilling, the instruction becomes maimed; and for this reason a life tempered with sadness and bitterness is useful. And on account of this. For this reason, then, a remedial and salutary medicine is found, which is repentance, that is to say, changing one’s mind and the condemnation of the matter. To this, indeed. That is, to these things, both deeds and words stretch out their hands, or [they do so] as to the causes and principles.
Lifeless. Powerless. Maimed. Incomplete.
Of the signal. Of your will, of your consent.
The mixtures of things. The state of having pleasant things mixed with painful ones. C 
Rash. Irrational.
Of joy. Of pleasure.
Beyond merit. Beyond what is expected, and said by the many to be outside what is decent.
Calamities. Just as many other expressions were understood in different significations in former and bygone times, and differently used by later and lesser ones, so also this word *calamity* [σῠμφορά], being originally a middle term, and understood even of that which is advantageous—as [in the verse]: *"For joys, tears creep from the eyes"*—is understood by those who came later only as something evil, and is censured beyond what is likely and beyond its merit. For in truth, calamities are not causes of evils, but [instruments] of training and prudence; on the contrary, the so-called good fortunes that are noised about are rather causes of evils. D 
They contribute. They work together.
For removing the feeling. The great and inherent commotion of the soul.
First providence. It is read in another manuscript, "the supernal providence." He calls the just method and dispensation of God, the creator of the world, "the first and supreme providence," distinguishing it clearly from others. For the Greeks, thinking the sun, moon, and stars to be ensouled, said that they also provided for earthly things from their own dwelling place.
For their sake. Of the calamities.
To the incautious. To those devoid of intelligence and thoughtless.
Of happiness. For since they are the causes of ruin, the many, being deceived, proclaim them to be happiness. A snare. An ambush and a trap against souls. To the overseers. From the demons who are the overseers. Let another say it. One who thinks in the Greek manner. To the one having entered. But I say, clearly. Is reached. Is given. Things here are sweet. The deceptive quality of matter.
Mild. What he says is of this sort: Since necessity, he says, demands that the soul serve the nature of the world, it descends from above like a hired servant, so that having served for a stated time it may receive its wage, as if it were a prize, and ascend whence it descended. For all things which have generation and growth require the soul; and this is the service and ministration of the soul, that it should serve the generation of things in the world, until there exists in that receptive body of each the power to carry a soul, and it is not troubled by old age or any wasting whatsoever and earth-born corruption, such as wander about life in many ways and many turnings. But for the most part, having been deceived by the enticements of desires and pleasures, as though intoxicated, it took falsehood in place of truth, and grasping a shadow instead of reality. A hireling. A paid servant.
First life. He says "first life," distinguishing it from the second and third, which the Greeks say come to pass because of the transmigrations of souls. It serves. Or it takes the rank of one bought with silver instead of a hired servant. Of Adrastia. Of fate. Having been seduced. Having wandered through pleasure. Of the gifts. Whatever things soothe and sweeten sight, taste, hearing, smell, and touch, he says are all gifts of matter.
Of gifts. Of the pleasures in the continuity of nature. Toward a composed state. Fixed. To serve. As if they had made a written agreement. Let us be pleased. Let us be gladdened. Of extraneous things. Spurious. To agree. To come to an arrangement in writing. But she. Adrastia is nature. Ineffable. Mystic and not brought forth through the agency of the tongue.
Let us deliberate. No one could know these things accurately who has not suffered beforehand and who has not first taken some experience of them; but those who are ensnared by the longing for children and wives might know it better. For being deprived of these at some time, or having left them of their own will, they suffer constant, rending sorrows, so to speak,
dissecting the soul night and day, which indeed he calls material punishments. For these conjure up the idol and phantom of the thing of which one has been deprived, and frequently bring it to the memory of the one who desires it; and like wild dogs, they tear at him bitterly from every side and drag him about. Many, indeed, they have driven to their death, not being able to endure the suffering with constancy.
Fugitives. Runaways.
He seizes upon. For this reason it is also called Adrastia.
Not trivial. Not small.
To delete. To frustrate.
Punishments, then. Passionate, punitive thoughts.
Material. Pressing upon the soul.
Then indeed. Some say that necessity, Adrastia, fate, and nature differ from one another; but others say that one thing is signified by these terms. For what a person cannot escape, this would be Adrastia and necessity; but fate is that which governs earthly things through the chain of the natural connection and physical interlocking of beings. And they say that earthly things are subject to fate when they operate bodily and naturally; but they are under providence when they operate intellectually, that is, when men receive blessings directly from God.
Of it. Of fate, which is also Adrastia and nature. B 
Called. By the ancients; hence temptations are named.
To endure. To suffer the things that befall one, and which are grievous.
Divine words. Those that appear mythical, but are mystical and more divine; for the myths seem to say that Eurystheus ordered these trials and necessities upon Heracles; but the mystical words, by unfolding the things said in an anagogical way, refer them to another meaning.
Another. Similar to Heracles.
Constantly. Strongly.
He attempted. He laid a hand and struggled strongly, so as to be freed.
The hands. Or as of this physical world, which is governed by nature.
The leap. The jump, as being weak.
And it is necessary. There is need.
He spares not. Matter.
As of alien things. As if they had bounded away from its own genuineness.
Of the jars. Homer in the Omega of the Iliad discusses such conversations with Priam, who was asking for his son Hector; it goes like this:
For two jars stand at the threshold of Zeus,
Of gifts that he gives of evils, but the other of goods. D
Jupiter, mingling for him, bestows [them] with his thunderbolt,
Sometimes he meets with evil, at other times with good;
But he to whom he gives of the baneful, he makes him a thing of scorn,
And an evil hunger drives him over the divine earth,
And he wanders neither honored by gods.
*Latently.* Concealed. *Parts*. For all material things, whatsoever are in this natural world constituted of generation and corruption, are divided in two ways. For to some of them, things which are good and desirable and pre-eminent fall by lot; to others, on the contrary, come evils. Except that the good are rare, while the evils are many. *To Jupiter himself.* That is, to Homer, at that part of the Homeric verses where he says this. *Lord of matter.* Since Homer makes Zeus the dispenser in these verses of the goods and the evils that the jars contained; and this is a matter of material service, as if destiny were commanding from a higher place, it seems that he makes him the lord and ruler of matter as well. *Of the double [gift].* Of the twofold, as of that which comes from the two jars. *In error.* In the soul. *Having returned.* But having become heavy and having remained below toward the variety of material affection. For this very material affection is that which prevents [the soul] from returning to the region of the supersensible worlds, from whence it also made its first descent.
*See.* I look upon, *God*. See. It is written *thee*, which is also better than *thee* [in the sense of] divine, being synonymous. *Is traded.* As will be said. *The account said.* The Egyptian and sacred. *Should happen upon.* Should fall into. *Dark-shining.* To that which is dark on both sides. *In a cloud-enveloped place.* The Egyptians and Chaldaeans call the world under the lunar sphere a cloud-enveloped place, dark on both sides. But they say that in the lunar sphere itself it is alternately covered and alternately luminous, that is to say, shining with one half of its part, and being without light in the other half. For indeed the moon itself is of such a nature, always having one half of its part turned toward the sun and illuminated, while the other is empty of the splendor of light, made by proportion according to its own constantly changing revolutions and circuits and various turns. But the place above the moon, up to the highest and all-encompassing sphere, they call the world luminous on all sides, that is to say, perpetually illuminated on both sides by permanent and immortal light. *Place.* To that which is terrestrial according to the soul. *Follows.* The phantastic spirit. *To the greatest extent.* Plato also says in the *Phaedrus* that this is the law of Adrasteia: whichever soul, having become a follower of God, catches sight of any of the truths, is to be unharmed until the next period, and if it can always do this, it is always unscathed, but if it, being unable to attain it, has not seen it, and having met with some chance fall, it becomes filled with forgetfulness and wickedness and is weighed down,
to be wingless; but when, having become powerless, it fails to see [the goal], and by some misfortune becomes filled with forgetfulness and vice and is weighed down; and having been weighed down, it sheds its feathers and falls to the earth, then the law prescribes this and that. Seek also what follows.
The "Opposite" arrives. "As far as the lunar sphere." For that which is opposite to it is the world which is on all sides dark, which reaches up to this point; but he speaks of it as "opposite" with respect to that which is surrounded by light and is on every side shining. Dross. By depreciating it thus, he speaks.
portion. And a lot.
But this one. The "all-luminous."
For not only. For he does not exhort us to lead up only the nature of the phantastic spirit to the upper world through a fitting purification, but also if it had taken anything from the fringes of fire and air—which indeed he called the dross of matter—he commands that this also be carried up together.
The "eidolic." The phantastic.
The earthly. Which previously he called a bony shell, now he calls it the body, a terrestrial integument.
The divine. He calls the phantastic spirit the "divine body," which above he called the first body of the soul.
And into one. Into a single nature having co-existed. On the cliff. He calls it the dross of matter, which the phantasia, descending from the upper spheres, begged from the natural elements of fire and air. He says, therefore, that this also should not be left on the cliff—that is, in this world surrounded by darkness—but must be raised up through temperance and the other virtues, and led up to the ethereal world; since it exists by nature, and for the idol, there is a portion in a place that is luminous on all sides. He calls the phantastic spirit an "idol," as connatural with the rational soul, but being inferior to it. For just as the intellect is an idol and image of God, and the rational soul of the intellect; so also the irrational soul, that is, the phantastic spirit, is an idol and image of the rational soul. Since, therefore, nature—that is, the natural soul—is an idol of this phantastic spirit, and the body of this in turn, and again matter of the body; and so phantasia, he says, has a certain relational proportion toward those things which from both sides have a bond of communion and affinity with it, and tend together toward the same end. Wherefore he instructs it not only from the rational soul upward, but also downward from the natural elements, of which he speaks, descending and begging [them]—I mean fire and air.
From neighbors. And especially those, he says, have a relationship for whom the position of the region is from neighbors and is near, and in succession—just as the elemental fire is near and in succession, by way of continuity, to the celestial and ethereal body, and they are not separated as the earth is separated from the aether, which is the last and lowest of those that exist.
Fire: The elementary.
If the better things. If the soul, although it is better than the body, has nonetheless accepted communion with the body, and has acted in concert with it through the medium of the imaginative [faculty], and has shared with the dregs some portion of the better and uncorrupted nature, in as much as it has partaken of the worse; it would not even accept the body, unless it hindered the imagination and drew it away from the soul’s natural union, but rather provided itself as docile and obedient to it, so that it might be led back to a more divine and higher condition. For just as the imaginative soul, inclining towards somatic affections, becomes thick and moves toward a more somatic condition, so also the body, inclining toward contemplation through the medium of the imagination, may become attenuated and may be etherealized along with the rational soul, passing beyond the extremity of the elements, which it called a "misty region" above. He speaks of that which extends from the earth to the lunar sphere as being composed of the four elements. For from the lunar sphere begins the substance and nature which moves in a circular motion up to the highest and first sphere, the all-encompassing one, which he also calls "bright." The older Greeks, however, used to burn the very bodies of the dead, just as if they were placing these too, along with the soul, in the ethereal region.
If the better things. These are the rational soul and the faculty of sensation, which have withdrawn from the material elements.
Acted in concert. Coexisted.
Dregs. He means the soul and the phantastic spirit.
When it has yielded. By matter.
The worse things. Either as fire and earth, or those from which the body is composed.
But docile. This word is Platonic. For he says the chariots of the gods are docile and obey the reins, and therefore easily travel their course.
Following. Accompanying.
The middle. The phantastic.
Of the first. Of the rational soul.
Portion. Lot.
Of the elements. The wiser of the Greeks interpret the myths concerning Athena in this way, and they say that Athena is the soul, and Zeus is the air; they say she is the daughter of Zeus, having been born from his head, that is, from the summit of heaven, and that she is simple and incorporeal. They call her "Tritogeneia," because upon descending she receives the spirited part from the aether, and... the moon, for its nature is moist and flowing; for this reason the impostures of lusts...
occur toward it. Then, she receives the composition of the body from the elements in order. These things, then, the Greeks assert, but the Egyptians and Chaldeans say other things; but since Synesius sees them thus disagreeing, he says that one must withhold belief from them in part, and in part grant it.
*Corporeal substance.* See, again he says that the phantastic spirit is a corporeal substance, which he had called above the first body of the soul, and again a divine body, and yet again an untainted body.
*Substance.* That is, the phantastic spirit.
*It is altogether impossible.* It is very difficult in consideration of its dignity.
*Let it be raised together.* Or, let it ascend together.
*And to the celestial spheres.* From which it first descended.
*Ultima (Extremes).* Wishing also to speak about the intermediate regions, he recapitulates what has been said and puts it in order. He says, therefore, that these are the two ultimate limits or extremities of the universe: one, the lower, which is shrouded in darkness, and one, the upper, which is entirely radiant; the one is miserable, and the other is blessed. But since opposites cannot be joined immediately, he says that there are also other regions in between these two limits, many and various, which he calls "heterophae" (differently radiant) and "heteroneph" (differently dark), meaning that they partake of the limits on either side and are partly radiant—for instance, just as there is a middle between the intellect and the soul, and in the middle of the soul and the imagination is opinion, and between the imagination and the body is sense-perception. And these are the middle grounds extending through one another from the body up to the intellect, and they all participate in some way in the things bordering them on either side. Just as the air, having two extremities and termini on either side, is warm in its upper terminus, which approaches the elemental fire, but is moist in its lower terminus, near the water.
*The extremities.* The critical points.
*Regions.* Other tracts and places.
*Of the world.* Around the lunar sphere.
*Soul.* I think, while still being in the body.
*Of the spirit.* Of the phantastic (spirit).
*Forms and manners.* Which seems to happen already in the case of men who live purely, blamelessly, according to virtue, and intellectually. For these, even though they are on earth, possess a mind that travels the aether.
*To its own.* As to its own proper terminus, the one that is entirely radiant.
*Having fallen.* To the lower one, which is shrouded in darkness.
*And it becomes indefinite.* It escapes and spreads into the variety of matter.
*In the middle, however.* Or, as in the middle terminus and region, which is "heteroneph" and "heterophae."
*Of the ones.* Of the blessed and the miserable limit.
To discern. Namely, you might affirm that the soul itself is a certain wonderful nature, that is to say, it participates in the superior [nature] to the extent that it surpasses what is baser in matter. In any order. Of the middle, interjected. Or entirely. Absolutely. It is divine. Perfectly, integrally. Of the divine. The very small.
Now, that which is fallacious. That phantastic element, the wandering and lying [part] in predictions, is "alecton"—that is, it has no fixed end or place; so one must understand it as the absurd, having an affect and ignoble ambition, and of those rolling, that is, wallowing, in matter. And Aristophanes the comic poet says in the play of the Clouds, as if from Phidippides dreaming and saying: "Drive the horse away after having rolled it home," that is, "Drive the horse home, having prepared it to be rolled."
Now, that which is roaming. A kind of demon that makes its predictions while wandering.
It is "alecton." Having no fixed end or place.
Of those wallowing. The Chaldeans say that the demons who wallow in matter and earth are beasts of the earth; and they say that such beasts will inhabit our life, which has become full of passions. For such kinds of demons are substantiated in the passions, and have their seat and material order. Therefore, even those who are subject to passions come toward these and are as if glued to them, drawing like to like, being moved and possessed of power for agitation from the passions.
Of wallowing. Of rolling demons.
For in this way. Thus through repentance.
The *sierraion*. He calls the *sierraion* the nature between the blessed and the miserable lot, which he had also called a demonic nature a little before. He says, therefore, that this demonic nature thus creeps in both to God and to an older demon, being a neighbor; yet it flies to higher things, and takes hold of the region in the higher limit prepared for the pure and more divine nature.
By nature. These things are in keeping with the opinion of Plato, and no less with that of Plotinus. For Plotinus says that all men from the beginning used sense before mind and intelligence, and having first associated with the things that fall under sense, they considered these things to be the first and the last; and thinking that what was painful or pleasant in them was, on the one hand, evil, and on the other, good, they thought it sufficient if they pursued the one and averted the other. And this seemed wise to them, according to the heavy ones among the birds, which, having taken much from the earth and become weighed down, are unable to fly on high; but those that have hung themselves a little from the lower things, moving themselves from the pleasant toward the better of the
soul, having been unable to see the proceeding of the soul on high, they have been brought back again to actions in the name of virtue. But a third genus of divine men, by a better power and the sharpness of the eyes, saw the brilliance on high, and was raised there, as if above the clouds of the mist here, and remained there, looking down on all things here, having found rest in a place that is true and akin, as a man arriving from some great wandering into a well-ordered fatherland.
Constitution and state. We might conjecture his condition, of what sort it is; for to the man to whom it pertains, we might suspect and comprehend. The phantastic. A pure phantastic sees some of the things that are to be. Conjecture.
As a guess and a promise from conjecture.
Of the soul. As far as can be from the shape and habit of the soul. Better. That he ends in a happy lot. Not least. But most of all, he says, we hunt and overtake from the phantasms, which the phantastic spirit projects in sleep, that is, about what it is occupied, and we conjecture in what state and disposition it happens to be, just as he had previously said, that oracles harmonize regarding it— C stating, when it is least of all aroused externally by something else, such as from some bodily weakness or bitter calamity. For just as those who are thirsty in their sleep seem to dwell around rivers and fountains of water, so also those who are freezing [seem to dwell] near fires and warm baths; and those who have a more moist belly, [near] marshy and muddy and damp places. Similarly also those who have been bereft of child or spouse by death seem to see them in their sleep, because the prattling of the reasoning faculty persuades the phantastic to spend time on such things. By another. That is, when the phantastic is not troubled by an occurring passion, such as, for instance, by love or by fear or by fever, or by any other weakness. For such people are deceived both when awake and when asleep. For the lover, from some small similarity, seems to see the face of the one he loves. And the coward likewise thinks he is being pursued by enemies; and those suffering from fever likewise, in a confusion of the moving sight, think they see animals painted on the walls. Such movements, therefore, he now forbids, and does not admit them into the pure judgment of the phantastic.
Animal. Phantastic. Of philosophy. Of a philosopher and a temperate way of life.
As. Like.
Application. That which is appropriate and fitting.
Projection. Progress.
Power. According to power.
Anticipating. Conquering, cutting off in advance.
Uncontrollable. Inconceivable.
Worse. By the body.
As many as. Moderate sleep, water, and a little bread.
Application. Contact, apprehension, or as contemplation.
Coexisting. Or as those rising up.
Toward the spirit. According to the most destructive aspect of the phantastic.
Spirit. God.
Just as when. For just as certain things, being resolved and thinned, are stretched out and broadened, and occupy more space, as also those solid bodies which are evaporated into vapor; so, on the contrary, it happens with those that are gathered and thickened. For they are contracted into a smaller volume and occupy less space than before, just as air, when thickened and becoming water, is drawn together into a smaller volume than before, as also when water is frozen into ice. Thus therefore, he says, the phantastic spirit, having been fashioned by the Creator, is sufficient for filling the cavities of the brain; if, however, by some incidental thickness it is contracted into a smaller volume, certain parts of the cavities of the brain are said to be empty. Then, since it is not possible by nature for there to be any vacuum among things that exist, it follows of necessity that some evil spirit must enter into such empty and evacuated places, by which it is necessary that the animal, together with its imagination, be afflicted with harm.
Gathered. Contracted into a smaller volume.
In things that exist. For it is a philosophical dogma that there is no vacuum.
Full. Filled.
Penalty. Condemnation.
That. Which he called intellectual application.
Of piety. The perfection of divinity.
We indeed. In this place, by making a repetition of what has been said, he reveals also the purpose of the book: that we had set out to speak concerning the divination of dreams, yet, being compelled by necessity, we have also held a long discourse concerning the phantastic spirit as being a leading and necessary thing to be mentioned.
Applying ourselves. Attempting.
Upon this. Beyond this.
We have investigated. We have discussed more curiously.
Of the word. Of the proof.
Here. Concerning divination.
He has revealed. It has been made manifest.
Of the spirit. Of the phantastic.
Of piety. Of divine administration. That it is. The phantastic spirit. On account of such. On account of divination. From being inflamed. Luxuriating. Unfortunate. Cheap, sober. At Pytho. One. It is necessary. It is lacking. He however. This one. And that. On account of the desire for divination. To love. To. Joined. To Him, to God. Standing alongside. Following. For it is not even on account of the soul’s reconciliation to God that the need for divination would then be lesser and deserving of neglect; but as long as man exists, he requires even this. Of the better things. Just as the moon, being illuminated by the sun, has the surface of its face turned towards us light-filled, and is not mingled with it on account of the magnitude of the distance; but when it begins to turn towards it, it is already illuminated into its depths and made bright, mingling its own dim light with the great light from the sun on account of its closeness to it: so also our soul receives rays from the ruling mind, or rather from God; but only superficially when it turns towards sensible things; yet through the depths when, turning back, it draws near to Him. Then, therefore, he says, it is not entirely turned away from the living creature, but it turns also to sensible things, and from a vantage point it clearly considers them more purely, because it is not mingled with them, nor confused by them. The things below. The things in the world. Being with them. When it is more material. So that remaining. The soul is undistracted from matter. Specters. Likenesses and prototypes. Descending. Just as the light of the sun passing through filthy places is not defiled. Better. The mind. Worse. The body. To children. To my own. Having prepared. Or as having taken varied and abundant provisions. To Pytho. To Delphi. Of Ammon. They call Ammon Zeus. Now there is a temple of Ammon lying between the Nile and Libya, to the west of the Nile, but to the east of Libya and Cyrene; and it is more to the south than the Egyptian sea, at a distance of 1500 stades. To fall asleep. To recline. Homer in the Odyssey speaks of Penelope, when she is already about to...
He sees indeed. The republic and civil administration of the Athenians being divided into four orders, they called one that of those who possessed a census of five hundred medimni; another that of the knights; another that of the zeugitai; and the fourth that of the thetes. This thetic order was also the most indigent, always performing daily labor for wages. These orders and census valuations the Athenians were wont to speak of. So that he may survive. Scarcely living. Rower. By means of a small boat. The thete. The mercenary. The one who pays. It was a custom among the Athenians to receive every year twelve drachmas from foreign visitors who gathered in Athens for the sake of residence, and this was called the "tax of the sojourner" (metoikion). Sojourner's tax. Concerning those who are moved from abroad to Athens. Eteobutades. The Eteobutadae were an ancient, distinguished, and illustrious clan in Athens, from which the priestesses of Minerva were chosen. But it is etymologized from *eteon*—that is, the true—meaning those who are truly descended from the lineage of Butes; and the tragic poet Aeschylus calls the all-seeing Argus "Butes," or as a herdsman, as one presiding over and following Jo, the daughter of Inachus. For he brings her in saying thus: "I tremble, beholding the many-eyed Butes." Popular. Familiar, social. To be present everywhere. Since he has mentioned certain histories here, it is worthwhile to explain them briefly for the sake of clarity of speech. Near Antioch of Syria is a certain spring named Castalia, in which they reported that Apollo prophesied in this way: at the outflowing of the water a breath was given off, which at different times produced a different agitation of the water, which the seers who inspected it used to foretell future events. So this is one history. C Another is that concerning the rock of the Delphians. This is located in the province of Phocis, three hundred stadia more to the south than Mount Parnassus, and some two hundred stadia more to the north than the Corinthian Gulf. There was a temple there near a certain rocky mountain, and in it was the prophetic tripod of Apollo. The third history is that concerning the chasm of earth which he mentions; and it is as follows: There was a certain man named Trophonius, a seer by profession. This man, led by vanity, threw himself into a certain cave and, having hidden his life there, passed away. This cave, he says, used to render oracles to those who entered and inquired about anything whatsoever. D Synesius mentions these histories here for the greater praise of the divination that occurs through dreams, saying that those other places of prophecy required the greatest labors, hardships, and expenses, but this method of foretelling grants oracles to all without labor. And not to occupy. To not dwell in one place, or as, or at the water of Castalia, or if—
one is at the stone of Pytho, or at the underground oracle of Trophonius, or whatever other of the local oracles.
First. Better. Component. Having the purpose of seeing a dream. For nature. For the rest of nature. Not being enough. Our essence, or nature, not being able to watch forever. The choice. The dream. For the necessary. For sleep. He says sleep is a work and necessary, and the cause of being and living. But the dream is a choice, and an accessory, and the cause of well-being.
Well-being. Living well through divination in sleep. B To be. To live. For someone. For an action. To it. For the action. You will be helped with difficulty. When, clearly, you are hindered. For it is not of any time. For he said previously that there are certain defined places subject to and suitable for prophesying, and they are few, and they provide the oracles at determined times; therefore he also says that such divination is for these reasons difficult; and further, it is difficult because of its instruments, which it needs for its service. For he said these also require frequent expense. For it would not be easy to collect a Cretan herb, and an Egyptian feather, and an Iberian bone, and whatever other things those who labor miserably over such instruments prattle and babble.
But for what causes. I would say, the instrument, namely. There are carriages. A wagon, a vehicle. Other parts. Words and deeds full of mysteries. For it is truer to say so. They revealed, namely. And they brought forth. Willingly. Divine revelation. Someone. Any man. By way of force. For the priests who seek external divination, of whom he spoke a little before, using magical art, through certain incantations draw down some demons, and keep them against their will tyrannically and violently, and inquire of them about what they need to learn. This is clear from the incantations by which they bring Hecate into the midst, saying: "After the morning splendor, filled with stars, I left the undefiled and vast house of God, and I ascend to the earth that nourishes the living by your warnings, and the persuasion of nefandous verses, by which a mortal man certainly charms the dull hearts of the immortals." And again, another demon, being forced, says, he says: "Hear me unwilling, since necessity has bound me." And again, another elsewhere says: "Why do you always call me down from the ether sitting here, having compelled Hecate the goddess with your divine necessities?" and the rest. For such natures of demons, having become crasser on account of deep-rooted wickedness, have acquired for themselves an irrational soul, and because of this, partly indeed—
nature, which indicates it regarding natural things. He called him Prometheus. And Aeschylus, too, in the drama which he entitles *Prometheus Bound*, introducing Prometheus distressed by the bond and recounting the causes for which he is punished by Zeus, says: "And I stopped mortals from foreseeing [their fate], having implanted blind hopes in them." But Euripides, being more intelligent, refers the name of Prometheus to the common creator of all things, clearly saying: "What then of Prometheus, whom they say fashioned us and all other living creatures, [and] granted to the beasts, to each according to its kind, a single nature? All lions are brave, [and] in turn all hares are timid." *At will*: To the purpose.
*Captain of the company*. A company is a military formation of sixteen men. Now, the one standing in the front and leading the company is called a captain (*lochagos*); but the one behind is the rearguard (*ouragos*). The company as a whole is called a file (*stichos*), and by some an *enomotia*. Some, however, have declared the *enomotia* to be the fourth part of the company, and its leader an *enomotarch*. Two *enomotiai* they called a double-company (*dimoiria*), and its leader a *dimoirith*. So that a half-company is also called a double-company, and the half-company leader a *dimoirith*. A syllogism is when another [premise] is placed beside the first company.
*Of the feet*. Of the fetters on the feet.
*Insomnium (Dream)*. Since this wise man Synesius, regarding the name of dreams and *insomnia*, it has seemed good to us to discuss these matters more extensively. For there are differences in what the ancients laid down concerning those things that appear in sleep. For they say there are five types of these: of which they call one an *insomnium* (dream), another a *phantasma* (apparition), another an *oraculum* (oracle), another a *visio* (vision), and the fifth a *somnium* (common dream). And an *insomnium* is properly the echo of daytime cares; this happens either when the soul is troubled in some way, or when the body has suffered something. The soul, for example, as when a master is provoked to anger at a slave, because he perhaps did not manage the assigned matters well and decently; then, while sleeping, he thinks he is arguing with the slave about the same things. It is just as we see happens on the sea, when the north wind has agitated it, and then when the blasts of the winds have ceased and quieted down, the turmoil of the sea remains. In the case of the body, it is as if someone, oppressed by thirst, imagines he is running beside rivers and springs. *phantasma* is when a person is neither fully asleep nor fully awake, but, being placed as if in the middle ground and interval between these, he imagines he sees strange forms, unusual in nature, variety, and magnitude, either all together or few at a time, moving about in various ways, and bringing either turmoil or joy. An *oraculum* is when either an angel, or a priest, or a father, or someone of those having dignity, is seen and addresses the person, bringing either danger and grief to some...
rendering [the matter] as though it were good both the sailing together and the doing [of it], and the accomplishing of all these things for all people, which I mentioned a little before, but the goods [that came] to me personally from her [the art] I have not yet made public.
148. *To one inquiring.* If a dream is a dream. And [the word] *one*. Meaning. *Of names.* Of poetic [names]. *Of the ancient.* He speaks of Ancient Attic, which began from Gorgias, whose leader was that Gorgias the Leontine: for he used also poetic names for the sake of gravity and novelty of speech. But the New Attic began from Aeschines and Demosthenes; and this was smoother and more pleasant to the ear. *And the same.* *Something.* That an action or each. *What it is said.* Having also added the quality, that it is good. *Born as.* Surpassing. *To the mistress.* To a fixed day. *To wait at the door.* To remain at the door; from *aulizomai* (to lodge). *Fortune.* Immediately. *I have been an ambassador.* If the embassy to the emperor Arcadius had not interrupted me at some point. *And yet.* Nevertheless even then.
*Of those who summon spirits.* The science of summoning souls and the evocation of the dead mean the same thing. Thus, indeed, one and the same matter is expressed by many names among the Greeks, just as not a few other things are. But what is signified is such: some people, using certain impostures and incantations, do other monstrous and prodigious things, and they contend that they can bring up the souls of whichever of the dead they wish. They appear, then, to summon souls through certain incantations and adjurations; for they appear and answer regarding the things asked of them. But the things that appear are not the souls of the dead, but certain demons, which indeed were formerly angelic and luminous ranks, then, on account of their swelling, pride, elevation, arrogance, and wickedness, they were once cast out from that divine choir and company, and are now wandering around this sublunary region, having cast off at the same time both that luminosity and that subtlety, and having changed instead toward darkness, and toward a thickness that is diverse and multiform.
The wise men of the Greeks and Chaldeans say, therefore, that some of them are aerial, some watery and marine, some earthly, and others subterranean; and that some of them delight in the odors [of sacrifices], others in blood, others in certain pollutions, and others in other things, and different ones in different things. For this reason, also, the oracular Apollo commands those who pursue necromancy to use some sacrifices, and others for those who practice hydromancy, and others for aeromancy, and others for others. We, however, leaving most of these aside, let us move on only to the words of the necromancers
who use the magical art of divination around the corpses of the dead. And the oracle-monger Apollo indeed exhorts them to practice these things: "Slaughtering victims," he says, "bury them for the powers below, and into a trench let blood flow. Pour out honey for the nymphs, and the gifts of Dionysus." These are the same things that Homer’s Circe instructs Odysseus to do when he is descending into Hades:
When you have reached this spot, hero, moor your ship,
And dig a trench, and measuring it with your elbow,
Draw it out in length, and let it be wide enough.
There, as a suppliant, pour libations to all the shades:
First with honeyed syrups, and then with liquid mead,
Then with the sweet and bubbling wine of sacred Lyæus,
And third, let the water of the sea be added to them.
Add to these the white flour you have ground,
And bending your knees, worship the unwarlike shades,
And call upon the vanishing souls with your vows:
That if it be permitted to return to the ancestral home of Ithaca,
You will sacrifice a barren heifer,
Whichever excels, which the whole herd surpasses,
And that you wish to heap up the built-up pyre with generous fruit,
And in the fortress of Laertes in sea-girt Ithaca.
Vow for Tiresias a sheep with a dark fleece,
Whichever wins among the flocks that graze in the pastures.
The meaning of these things is as follows: honey, because it presents itself as a rival, so to speak, to the bitterness of death; milk, on account of the dark and black color of, the shadows; wine, because of sorrows and troubles; and finally water, since man is composed of four elements. Further, he mentioned a sheep because those animals that walk on four feet are common to those of the earth and those below; it is black because of the gloom of that place. As for the sword, it is because those who employ demonic incantations are accustomed to hold swords in their hands, lest perchance the demons, violated by the exorcisms and evocations, should suddenly rush in a fit of rage and inflict some evil upon the one exorcising and calling them forth. For this reason, therefore, they are compelled to carry swords, so that by their means they may terrify them and not allow them to approach, but rather compel them to answer from a distance when questioned. For since they are vital spirits and possess density and thickness, as we have said, they themselves also fear cutting and division; they are even distressed if they are disturbed, struck, and beaten. For indeed, in humans who are struck and in pain, it is not the flesh that endures the labor and sickness, but the vital spirit. Otherwise, it would still feel pain even when separated from the life-giving soul that produces vital spirits. Nevertheless, those who elicit the shades and awaken the souls of the dead from the underworld, using the sorceries and evocations we have enumerated, bring and entice such demons as messengers, as it were, to bring back the souls of certain dead persons whom they wish. But since they are not able to bring back the souls by necessity, it remains for those demons to transform themselves into the forms of those deceased persons, and they are seen and recognized by friends and relatives, and they answer what is asked, and perform what is commanded. For they are often ordered to proceed to the harm of certain people, and they do so. The wise Synesius himself was once in danger of suffering such harm C
from certain hostile necromantic sorcerers, but he foresaw it while dreaming and thus immediately avoided the danger. He mentions this now in praise of the divinatory art, which exerts its power in the interpretation of dreams.
*Which it possesses.* For the imaginative soul contains species in accordance with its own domestic and peculiar nature; indeed, as he stated above, the soul possesses the species of things that come to be and happen, just as the mind possesses the species of things that truly exist.
*To those who are turned.* Having thus far designated two worlds—one luminous on every side, the other dark on every side—and having stated that the shining one is superior, as well as the orders and divine powers within it, which in the Chaldean manner he calls the gods, he follows up by saying the following, which will indeed be made clear if we first set forth the Egyptian and Chaldean opinions which he himself observes and imitates. For just as Moses says that man was made in the image and likeness of God, so also do the Chaldeans and Egyptians declare that the parent and creator of the world has sown certain symbols and indications of his own property into souls. For they say that not only do souls have their origin from the paternal seeds, but all the preceding orders have also sprung forth and had their prior origin there. Whence they exhort and admonish us that, since man was not produced from bodily temperaments into the nature of things, but had his substance and life from a higher and more ancient origin, namely from God, he also possesses a kinship and affinity with the supereminent orders. It is worth the effort for the same man to turn toward them, on account of the recollection of the more divine lot and inheritance which he received descending from the creator and parent of all things, so that he may ascend thither once more. Following these words directly, Synesius says that the soul, having rested from the tumult of vulgar and crass senses, bestows those species—that is, the recollections—which it admits and holds while descending from the supernal and heavenly region, upon those men who devote themselves to the contemplation of divine and eternal things. For it now has commerce with the more divine and supereminent orders, and a certain more divine power and faculty adheres to it familiarly from thence, from that world, precisely, which he calls the mundane God.
*From the same source.* From the same.
*Certainly these things.* Species.
*Whether by prudence.* He calls the virtue acquired by prudence that which springs up from experience; but he calls moral virtue, which arises from habit and teaching, that which is produced by habits. Aristotle, indeed, as if tracing the true origin of the word, names it "moral" from "custom."
