But custom is an activity, while character is the quality that is generated in the impressionable soul through activity, which is to say, the end and the work of custom. He says that none of the moral virtues is generated in us by nature. For nothing of those things that exist by nature can be trained to be otherwise; for example, fire, being by nature upward-tending, could never be trained to be carried downward, and other things likewise.
*Or by habits.* He means ethics, the science derived from a teacher and from habit. *To some other person.* To a man not studious. *Not for a slight one.* But for a great and necessary matter. *Of the best sort.* Just as it happened once to Balaam, a man of another race. *A class.* Of dreams. *Of things that have happened. Of things past.* *Since this, too.* The thing that will one day come into existence.
*Of existence.* The wise men say that the present world is a simulacrum of eternity; consequently, the things within it are also simulacra of the things that subsist in that [eternity]; and not only this, but they also call the imprints that originate from one another and relate to one another, simulacra as well. They say that the air is the subject capable of receiving all of these: for instance, as an example, the air receives the enunciated and articulated speech—not at all truncated—and, by shaping itself in an ineffable way, just like a mirror, it conveys it into myriad perceptions, dividing it while maintaining the parts in an unvaried manner; it does the same for all colors, all vapors, and for everything in the world that is subject to the sensory faculties. These, then, and such things, he calls the effluences of the idols of things that fall under sense perception, and the streams of composite things. For from the first and primordial matter according to the Greeks, from which the elements arose, what kind of effluence could there be, if it is formed only by conception? But we understand its effluence from the particular things in the world that are differentiated by composition. For these are always being born, being altered, and being corrupted. And not only according to the fluidity of matter, but, so to speak, these species also partake in the experience of this corruption, and thus, he says, the things in the world deny the dignity of that which truly is. The dignity of that which is, is to always be; but these things, on the contrary, are not always, nor are they able to stand in one state, but some have been, some are coming to be, and some are about to be; therefore, the idols flowing from all these things wander about and slip, hovering in the air; they press upon, and find some rest and repose in the fantastical spirit of the soul, which is itself an idol of the soul. For the soul is an idol of the intellect, and the fantastical spirit is, in turn, an idol of the soul. Therefore, he says, whatever idols flow from those things that have been, and from those that are coming to be, these perish clearly and plainly, but those that come from future things are indefinite, difficult to conjecture, and enigmatic. For just as if someone
passing by should see the flower of an almond tree, he would know immediately that the fruit of the almond will follow the flower; yet he would not know with certainty the qualities, and the magnitude, and the actual reaching of perfection of the fruit; so also all the images that flow from future things announce indeed that something will be; yet they do not, for the most part, [show] clearly the quality of the thing.
Images. Either as characters, and impressions, and types.
Of subsistence. Of essence.
Of matter. Either as coming to be from composite things; [the author] made "the flowing out" from the flowing away.
Captures. Either as it lays hold of and receives.
To flow out. To flow out from composite things according to matter, and form, both of which are changeable.
Of the being. Of that which is strictly being.
The things coming to be. The changeable things.
Of these. From [it].
Slipping away. Erring.
In indefiniteness. For they are types of beings and shadows, not in fact beings.
Of beings. Of those that are strictly beings.
To be recognized. As alien to the nature of those [beings].
In animal [spirits]. In the imaginative [spirits].
In nature. Human.
And those from these. Just as a house which is just arising and emerging is not yet [truly] being: for it has not yet come into a perfect existence; thus also Synesius conjectures here, and sets forth what he says. For example, he considers man, plainly, as long as he lives, as a thing coming to be; but when he dies, then he deems him to be perfected, and thus he is now "being" in act, as having ceased from coming to be, and having received the end of that which existed previously in potentiality.
Living. Living, surviving.
Of an imperfect [nature]. For that which is future is imperfect, because it has not yet come into being.
Leaping forth. From the metaphor of flowers which leap forth before the fruits.
From it. Read: upon it.
But now. From here he begins to teach how we ought to prepare ourselves for the receiving of the art of divination by dreams.
The spirit. The imaginative [faculty].
But nourishment. He teaches how one must nourish and increase the art of divination during sleep. He says that this is done through philosophy, that is, the meditation on death, and through a temperate way of life. For these are a bridle for the passions and a certain immobility. But an intemperate and licentious way of life, he says, produces a swell and a storm in the outermost body, which this [author], following Plato, called an oyster-like covering; and the body, on the other hand, according to...
succession, moves the sensory faculties, and these sensory faculties transmit the disturbance as far as the imagination; just as when someone disturbs water or air, and what has been disturbed in turn disturbs another thing, and that [disturbs] another, and this happens by succession for a long way. See also how he called the imagination the "first body": "first" because of the outermost and oyster-like covering, and "body" because it is thicker and more material than the soul.
Feeding. Upbringing. By which. By the passions.
The spirit. The imaginative [faculty].
By moderation. Through philosophy and simple diet.
The outermost. The fleshly; and he called it an oyster-like covering.
Of the first. Of the imaginative [faculty]. For above, too, he named the imaginative faculty the first body of the soul.
Of seafaring men. A seafaring man is someone sailing on the sea.
They are signified. They are indicated.
Of the same. We have often encountered the same things.
Signs of the weather. Storms, rains, lightning.
Of the encompassing [element]. Of the air.
Halo. Three, or two, or one.
 He discourses on the circles that appear like a halo around the moon, starting from somewhere above:
"If they encircle it [the moon] round about with a whole halo,
Whether three be set around it, or two, or if there be but one,
Expect both wind and fair weather—
[The wind] if it [the halo] is breaking; fine weather if it is fading, and so forth."
The meaning of the passage is this: If a single halo appears, expect fair weather, for the density dissipates little by little. But if it breaks, expect wind from that side whence the break occurs; and if two haloes should form, expect a storm, and an even greater one if there be three.
At the first. That is, at the one.
The reason. Of science.
The body. The fleshly.
The outermost. Above, Synesius has often named the imaginative spirit the first body of the soul, and an inviolate body, and divine; but [he calls] the flesh, along with the nerves and bones and the parts that grow with them, the outermost body and oyster-like covering; which indeed he also calls a gathering of the well-known elements, that is, of those which are so named by all men; these are the hot, the cold, the dry, and the wet. He says, therefore, that it is not possible to hand down an art for the imaginative spirit in this way, just as it is possible for this fleshly and outermost body of the soul in general, and [to provide] a single rule for dreams that appear in all sorts of ways. For the body, when it is stirred either by cold or by heat, is frequently affected in this way; but the imaginative spirit has infinite differences.
For one part, having become thickened, is likened to the nature of the earth; another to water; another to the air near the earth in its tenuity; another to the air above the clouds; another to the sphere of elemental fire; another to the ethereal. And one part is likened to the sphere, another to the sun, and so on similarly, as each part has thickness or tenuity or light-weight, according to the proper proportion.
*Lowest body.* This is the final arrangement of the elements.
*Arrangement.* It is not so regarding the imaginative [spirit].
*And reason.* The artful medical skill regarding men not yet created, nor yet drawing vital air.
*For usually.* And this passage is interjected up to: *not so by heat and cold.*
*Beyond nature.* Outside of custom.
*It is not hidden.* For it happens sometimes to some, a strange and newer infirmity.
*As a gnomon.* We apply this rule.
*One from another.* Imaginative from imaginative.
*By the greater.* Through that.
*Mixture.* Of thickness.
Timaeus intimates enigmatically.
Plato says in the *Timaeus* C that he who set up the universe divided souls equal in number to the stars and assigned each to each, and having placed them as if into a chariot, he demonstrated the nature of the universe; and he told them the fated laws, that a first generation would be ordained one for all, so that no one might be diminished, and the rest.
*To it, for its part.* To the Spirit.
*PAGE 152.* To the living creature. To the man.
*And to it.* To the Spirit.
*By itself.* By its own choice.
*For nothing.* For nothing else is so easily moved as spirit.
*Versatile.* Easily moved.
*Dissimilar.* Spirits.
*The same.* Similar.
*Of the same.* Images.
*But how could it happen?* The obstruction, he says, is manifold, and the failure of an accurate image in things that are contained in the imagination. For the different temperament in human bodies also shows different things that appear; and the different conduct, and manner of life, and the different nutrition, and the different time, and the different movement of those things being imaged. For, he says, clear water will show the phantasms of the things that appear more plainly than that which is colored and muddied; and even the muddied water itself, according to the differences of the colors and the pollution, will show different phantasms; and again, while the same water remains, its different movement will also itself cause a difference of the phantasms that appear. For either a phantasm would not appear at all, or else it would appear distorted and with difficulty. For such a movement...
disrupts the rectitude and truth of dreams. Therefore, he says, it is difficult in such varied and dissimilar things for a similar spectrum to be manifested, and yet more difficult to hand down a rule.
*Transparent.* Unclouded.
*That which remains.* Not moving.
*To be disposed.* To be fashioned, and to receive imprints.
*And the turbidity.* Or rather, the state of being clouded. For the clouding of white is one thing, and the clouding of that which appears black is another, and the clouding of that which appears red is yet another.
*In matters of configuration.* Either in the eddying or the straight-flowing motions of water.
*Whether then.* It seems that Synesius had also encountered the works of these men regarding the imaginative faculty and the art of divination.
*Melampus.* Melampus the Egyptian was a holy scribe, a hierophant, and an excellent seer. He it was who published, as they say, the sacred rites of Egypt to the Greeks, so that they might imitate them and also apply themselves to these things having been instructed by him; but Phemonoe was a prophetess of Apollo, using hexameter verses in uttering oracles.
*He will decree.* He will lay down doctrines.
*Let us question.* Let us ask.
*From dissimilar things.* For there are mirrors made of glass, and of iron, and of other material.
*Concerning the spirit.* Either they have not spoken concerning a difference of spirit, but as if all of it were of one nature—for they affirmed that it is prophetic, because it is prophetic in the faculty of imagination.
*To be a rule.* A teacher of the future.
*And a standard.* A correct guide.
*Of things differing in likeness.* For many, having drunk milk in a dream, experienced, when awake, the stillness of imminent tumults, all of them equally, although they were different people; and others again, having drunk wine, were troubled, being likewise different people, and countless other examples. Yet this does not happen in all cases and always, but in some cases and others, differently and otherwise.
*Likeness.* Similarity.
*Obscure.* From within.
*Being scattered.* Into different things in place of others.
*That regarding what erupts before its time.* The matter which is to happen after two years, if it appears today in the imaginative faculty, has erupted before its proper order, and has become a leaping forward of a thing not yet present, and a blossom of a nature as yet imperfect.
*But it is more difficult.* But it is, he says, more difficult to grasp and collect a common method for each individual human habit. For every man has a certain idiosyncrasy of opinion and knowledge. Therefore, phantasms are shown differently to different people, and different things become significatory to different ones, and not the same things for all. It is difficult, therefore, to collect and understand the phantasm that comes to pass differently in each person, and to declare it similar to a common phantasm—that is to say, similar to those of others—and to give a common and single teaching of these things to all men individually, just as perhaps we assign the common reason and definition of an animal to living creatures separately and individually, as to what is the substance
endowed with the faculty of feeling. But that these prognostications are revealed both while waking and sleeping according to the understanding, knowledge, and disposition of each person, and sometimes according to the art in which each is individually trained, is clear from what follows. For to the king of the Assyrians, Nebuchadnezzar, who was assiduously and eagerly occupied with the discovery and acquisition of all kinds of wealth, God revealed the future through a statue adorned and fashioned of gold and silver—the very material familiar to him. Furthermore, to the Magi, who were deeply engaged with the stars and their motions and stations, He revealed the mystery of the incarnate dispensation through a star. And to Abraham himself, when he was about to depart for Egypt, He revealed the things that were to happen to him through the art familiar to him; this was the Chaldean casting of nativities and the precise science of prognostication. For, "Take for Me," God says to him, "a heifer three years old, and a goat three years old, and divide them, and set the parts of the pieces opposite one another." And having done this, he foreknew that his seed would be a sojourner in a land not its own, and would be in bondage for four hundred years, and what follows.
*Phantasmati*. It is written also *phantasma* in some books, and in others *phantasmati*. For it was necessary that both be stated. But because of the awkwardness of the phrasing, one of the two is placed, whichever one might wish; however, it takes the other from outside, elliptically.
*Paraplesion*. Similar *phantasma*.
*Hexis*. Knowledge derived from habit.
*A kai*. Dreams.
*Sophon d' an*. It would also be wise in another way.
*Kainon*. The newer one.
*Agroikietai*. It would be disposed foolishly.
*Ephemerisi*. It is clear from this that this Synesius also wrote a book containing his own dreams, and all that followed upon such dreams of his. For these he calls *epinyktidas*, that is to say, observations of dreams occurring at night, and of the events that followed upon and accompanied them.
*En hekatera*. In the daytime and the nightly.
*Tes meses*. That which verges neither toward the better nor toward the worse.
*Hyph' hes*. Of observation.
*Egregorota te*. Just as Aristides the orator also did. For with his other books he compiled another book containing in detail his dreams, which he saw when he was overwhelmed by poor health. Philostratus the Lemnian, however, in his *Lives of the Sophists*, makes mention of this orator and says the following concerning him: "The Adriani produced Aristides—whether he be Eudaemonis's son, or Eudaemon." Now the Adriani is not a large city in Mysia. But Athens trained him at the time the age of Herodes flourished, and Pergamum in Asia, according to the interpretation of Aristocles' more obscure words. When
he was afflicted by an infirm and sickly constitution from a young age, he did not neglect labor, nor did he shrink from it. Therefore, he himself asserts in these divine books the nature of the disease and how his nerves became numbed with a shuddering. But when he is about to write these books, he applies and adapts to himself a certain discourse; for these diaries are good and useful instructors in how to discourse well on any subject. In this place, Synesius encourages everyone who has the power to write not to expend the labor of their tongue and style on other absurd themes, but to make as an exercise and a display those things which are seen during sleep, and to use such things as daily commentaries. He also makes mention of such things as Lemnian Philostratus records regarding the orator Aristides, rendering the audience more eager and ready through such testimony.
For rhetorical argument. For the faculty of polished speech, history, and the customary discourse.
By nature. For just as by nature bitterness is separated and divided from honey, just as by nature sweetness is in honey; but if anyone should see in dreams that he is eating honey, he will encounter sorrow and bitterness: and the contrary, if anyone eats wormwood.
By the word [reason]. According to the reason that possesses infinite imaginations.
To imagine. To teach about imagination.
Vile. Not small.
Of being. The sweetness from the honey.
In no way. The bitterness and the harshness.
For those who have thought of nothing similar unto themselves. For ignorant men.
With time. But the imagination, he says, bringing these things without any measure of time, shows them, not as many species—that is to say, as having a distinct variety—but all at once and as one.
And he might reach out. He gives to the imagination itself.
Dream. The vision.
We perish. We imagine.
Whatever it may be. For it often shows wealth or glory or a beautiful woman, testing, as it were, how each person stands regarding his judgment and his passion for material things.
He may wish. The imagination.
Most perfect. He calls the ancient Sophistry the most perfect rhetoric, which Philostratus the Lemnian calls "philosophizing rhetoric" in his Lives of the Sophists. For the ancient sophist, he says, discourses on those things in which those who indulge in the study of philosophy are occupied, with confidence of mind, as one who knows: and he makes the introductions to his speeches, "I know, and I understand, and I have long considered, and nothing is stable or certain for man." And such a form of introductions proclaims the nobility of the speeches, and magnanimity, and a clear grasp of reality. It is also like the divinely-inspired and oracular-sounding prophecy. For indeed the Pythian [Apollo] also makes most of his pronouncements in this way, saying, "I know the number of the sand, and the measures of the sea; and, the wall of the third-..."
of generation, Zeus creates a wooden work." The ancient sophistry used to discourse regarding courage, concerning justice, concerning heroes and gods, and how the idea of the world is fashioned.
Most Perfect
. Or, philosophy gathered from good habit, such as it was of old.
Juvenile daring
. Or, as making a test.
Giving way
. Passing by.
Of believing
. For someone thinks great things about himself regarding virtue; then, seeing in a dream what is dear to those who are passionate, he himself gapes just no less than they. The imagination, therefore, making a trial and testing, and conceding a little more than the opinion which one has about himself, proves to the man that he is not impassively disposed toward the visions, but either strongly assents, or strongly repels, as each happened to be occupying his reason with the matters of the day.
To believe
. Of opinion.
Effort
. Relations.
We loathe
. We hate.
Not in the least
. Or, as strongly.
Sleeping
. To us.
Most sweet
. More joyful.
To wipe off
. To remain.
Above life
. The daily one.
If, indeed, it should concern anyone
. If, therefore, someone should wish, he says, to record dreams in writing, he ought to compose them by means of names and verbs; whether, then, by words that sharply touch upon the underlying subject- matter and meaning, and clearly reveal the underlying mind; or otherwise, the study would be dead and foolish, being unable to lead the instructed listener into the same understanding and opinion such as he himself had and has, who is writing and teaching.
Inanimate
. Unintelligent and dead.
But of what sort
. Or, as for the sake of teaching the listener regarding dreams.
In the same place
. I say.
Passion
. Disposition.
Of those moving
. Of animate, living things, clearly revealing the underlying mind.
Conquers
. In dreams.
Everything makes room for
. It makes room for, he says, and the imagination easily teaches the sleeping one; but language would with difficulty make room, so as to teach the listener.
How, then, could it?
. Or, as not easily.
A dream
. An adverb, or as in sleep.
He sees a dream
. A noun, or as a dream.
He woke
. Still sleeping.
The Aloadae, then
. There were two sons of Aloeus and Iphimedia, Otus and Ephialtes, who, having become in height and body strength unbearable and unconquerable, put an end to the wars of their neighbors; and for this reason it was mythologized that they had bound Mars. It is also mythologized that, having thought even beyond human nature, they wished to ascend to heaven; for which reason they piled mountains upon mountains, upon Parnassus indeed Ossa, and upon Ossa, Pelion, and
in a similar fashion; whereupon they perished, struck by the lightning of Zeus.
*They indeed.* The sons of Aloeus, Otus and Ephialtes, are struck by the lightning of Zeus.
*Mountains.* Ossa and indeed Pelion. For these are mountains in Thessaly. Homer says, moreover, in the *Odyssey*:
They strove to set Ossa upon Olympus, and upon Ossa
Pelion with its waving leaves, that heaven might be climbable.
*Of Icarus.* Icarus was a man himself, and he wished to learn astronomy and the courses of the stars. But because he was not able to easily understand the demonstrations of the arguments, he gave up in the midst of his learning. For this reason, it was told in a myth that he fastened waxen wings to himself in order to fly to heaven; but in the midst of the flight, because the sun struck him more forcefully, the wings were melted, and he fell into that part of the sea that fell to his lot, from which the sea was also called Icarian (he therefore calls the flight of dreams more fortunate B ); and having fallen, he was drowned in the sea.
*Seeing.* The land.
*Signified.* Marked by a sign.
*Difficult.* To be.
*Said.* By day.
*Happens.* In dreams.
*For after a little bit.* Or as in some places it is found upon the earth. For he was not away, so that we might say he came upon the earth.
*For nothing is so.* For nothing is more proper to dreams than to steal away the intervening time and to show instantly things that are completed with time and labor by those who are awake, such as the acquisition of wealth, the luxury of houses, the abundance of wisdom, verbal discourse with angels, and all such things.
*Bleating.* The voice of sheep.
*Voice.* Articulate and perfect.
*Of arguments.* Those that appear in dreams.
*Words.* To write books.
*For I think.* For even in dreams, other living creatures frequently appear to be conversing with humans, and plants to be shifting of their own accord, and humans to be flying like birds, and changing their form into irrational animals, and infinitely many other monstrous spectacles. He asserts, therefore, that those who composed the myths from the beginning received their prompts from dreams. For just as the license and power of dreams display strange sights, a faint impression and an enigmatic image of the truth, and, so to speak, the preliminary sketches of things that will happen in reality, so too do myths, with a certain license, fashion plants, stones, and irrational animals conversing in human voices, and ten thousand such things, in which indeed lie hidden certain vague outlines and seeds of true exhortations; while economically displaying the phantom of pleasure to the greedy ears of the many.
*To which also a peacock.* To the myths.
*Toward the autonomy.* Power, authority.
*Preparation.* Exercise, prose composition. *To which commencement.* To men. *Of the art.* Which is a preparatory exercise. *End.* To this, the scope of the study. *As in.* As if they were practicing in vain upon the myths. *Facility.* Ease of a life without labor. *The diction.* But the diction, he says, would be something highly labored here for those who wish to write with ambition regarding dreams. He calls the words the "appendices" of things, inasmuch as they follow the things for the sake of their explication, just as a skiff follows a ship. *May it be.* The rhetorical kind of diction. *Slackening.* Descending from the height of philosophy, when one might wish to entrust some theory to a writing. *To practice.* As Aristides in the *Against the Four*. *To a wealthy man.* As Libanius in the *Declamations*. *Were.* They existed. *Talents.* Pounds. *Necessary things.* Friends to them. *Distributing.* Afterward. *Not that.* Not only not friends. *Citizen.* Such problems are often proposed to one another by rhetoricians for the sake of exercise; and most of all by Hermogenes of Tarsus. B Libanius used to meditate upon such things, and would exercise his style in them, extending the whole argument to a great length, amplifying it, composing it, and practicing it as if it were a shadow-fight, and contending in this manner. Synesius therefore remembers here one of those problems; it is as follows: A poor man and a wealthy man were enemies; the wealthy man promised that he would feed the citizens if the poor man were put to death; the people agreed. But the wealthy man did not feed the children of the poor man, and they died of famine; and he is judged for murder. Such a problem is meditated upon according to the letter and the sense. *Nonagenarian.* Here he seems to censure the sophist Libanius, who, having arrived at extreme old age, considered it worth the effort to invest his study in declaiming; for no one else’s meditations are found to be more numerous than his, nor as many. But meditation, he says, is a certain shadowed description and a preceding exercise for some work. But he who continuously practices the shadow of a work as if it were the work itself, unwittingly and secretly seizes a falsehood in place of truth, and labors in vain. *Preparation.* The shadow. *Pancratium.* They say there are five Olympic contests: boxing, wrestling, sprinting, the javelin, and the discus. But the *pancratium* was something else. For it was composed of boxing and wrestling. For it was permitted to the one who wished to engage in the *pancratium* to use not only the laws of wrestling, but also those of boxing for the sake of victory. Here, therefore, Synesius abusively uses this
word, and says that to "pancratiate" is to hold the mastery in every respect and to conquer; just as he elsewhere uses other words abusively. For example, in the epistle to the bishops regarding Andronicus, where he says: "I was predicting that Andronicus would return to his nature from any chance cause; nor is it likely that he will be more timid, because of this and that." For here he takes the word "more timid" for "more audacious."
*Pancratium*. As if to carry off the palm from all kinds of contests.
*To derive benefit from themselves*. That is, from their own indigenous orations and private commentaries, as if they themselves ought to benefit from their own studies and wisdom.
*Archilochus*. This Archilochus was an ancient lyric poet, who did not only sing to the lyre, but also described in heroic verse those things which were done in his own age. Philostratus also praises him in the *Lives of the Sophists*. For he introduces one of the sophists saying that Homer is the voice of the sophists, but Archilochus is the spirit of the sophists.
*Neither indeed*. Just as Libanius in his declamations.
*The heroic [genus]*. He calls the heroic genus the Greeks who set out against Troy, whom, he says, the poets made more famous and illustrious by their own poems, while they allowed themselves to be inglorious and unpraised, saying nothing about their own life.
*Heroic*. The heroes.
*More glorious*. More famous.
*Of virtue*. Of poetry.
*Being illegally done*. Being innovated.
*Let it be entrusted*. Time.
FRAGMENTS FROM THE UNPUBLISHED DIALOGUE OF NICEPHORUS GREGORAS TITLED "FLORENTIUS" OR "ON WISDOM"
(Excerpted from the Royal Manuscript of Paris; published by Val. Parisot in *Cantacuzène, homme d’État et historien*, Paris, 1845. We retain the analysis written in the vernacular by learned men (*).)
The *Florentius* is one of those dialogues which Gregoras liked to compose and in which he imitates the manner of Plato and Lucian, but even more so that of the latter, while flattering himself that he reproduces the bearing and style of the former. His goal in this instance is to recount, in a redacted form, A a public colloquy he held with Barlaam in 1330 or 1331, in which it appears the advantage remained with him. He himself has made mention of his account of this species of tournament of erudition in his *History* (XI, 10), and he assures us that he wrote it only at the solicitation of his friends. He disguises therein the names of the principal personnages under pseudonyms: thus he himself becomes Nicagoras (a pseudonym that presents both the beginning of his first name and the end of the second); one recognizes Metochites in Metrodorus; it is probably Synadenus (unless one suspects Cantacuzenus himself) who is Demaratus; the two parties (for Andronicus III and his grandson) are named Heraclidae and Cecropidae; in the same way, the place names (Athens for Byzantium, Calydon for Durazzo?, the anonymous city of Thessaly for Thessalonica?, etc.); and likewise "ostracism" for simple banishment. The dialogue is truly very piquant in more than one respect; and it offers interest for literary history. It is astonishing that it has not yet been published. One will be grateful to us for extracting from it a few passages which, even while mitigating the exaggerations of Gregoras, make one grasp well the two dominant traits of the character of Barlaam: a great presumption and a great frivolity, which, moreover, does not prevent his having combined in reality, with knowledge on various points (without being encyclopedic as he pretended), a certain facility and eloquence.
Here is how Barlaam is introduced (after ten or twelve pages of remarks relating in part to political affairs)—FLORENTIUS: A certain Xenophanes, the son of Thrasymachus, arrived in Greece, promising to give a great demonstration of wisdom; and first, he resided in Calydon, a great city of the Aetolians; and from there he went to another city, great and yielding to none of the foremost in discourse, as many as originate from the Aetolians, extending as far as the Locrii of Ozola. CRITOBULUS: From where— A and what lineage? — FLORENTIUS: Do you know, my friend Critobulus, a certain gulf called the Tarentine, both narrow and extending to a great length, and a great city situated at its northern cape, a colony of the Lacedaemonians? — CRITOBULUS: If you mean some other one, I do not know, Florentius; but if the one beyond the Ionian gulf, I know it very well. — FLORENTIUS: This Tarentine gulf, then, my friend Critobulus, towards the west and the Zephyrus wind releases what is called the second Greece, the one that was long ago a partisan of the philosophy of Pythagoras; but to the east, the province of the Calabrians, which, to one going to the *right* to the Iapygian promontory, joins with the Ionian Sea: from which that wise man, Xenophanes, has come. Yet he uses a dialect by nature Latin and Italian, but by force, also this very Greek, which is our native tongue. He, then, that Xenophanes—to tell it beginning from the top—was reported to us to be by lineage from Calabria, my excellent Critobulus, but that he had drawn his wisdom not from Calabria, but by going into the deeper parts of Italy, and not all or every kind of wisdom, but only that of Aristotle, as much as pertains to natural things and as much as pertains to syllogisms—since that is also very eagerly studied by the Latins and Italiotes there, not according to
Fragments from the dialogue titled "Florentius"
...Aristotle, but as it exists long ago transferred into his own constrained language. As a lover of the naturally beautiful, the man came to have a longing to relearn the Greek tongue; and from there, having moved to the Calydonians, he had everything refashioned there—language, beard, clothing, bearing, and character. For he no longer judged that he needed to shave his beard, nor to be clothed in Latin attire while among the Greeks with whom he was about to mingle, a foreign tribe; but rather, that he might not be disbelieved or suspected by anyone, he even went so far as to darken his robe. From there he moved to a certain Thessalian city, which claimed for itself more wisdom than its neighbors, situated not less than two hundred stadia from Athens and Attica. Having waited there for a significant time, he came into a clearer habit of the Greek language... And he, through this acquisition of the Greek language, having climbed high, thought himself to be a great man and pregnant with all wisdom; and he was looking down upon others as if from a watchtower, as they wallowed in some base and lowly life. But when he heard that Athens, the mother-city of all wisdom, was being feebly administered at that time regarding discourse, due to the misfortune involving Metrodorus and Nicagoras, and the oblivion and silence that had long ago been passed upon it, he was the more moved toward a more rash way of reasoning. He thought he finally had the opportunity, with no one opposing him, to depart for Athens and become a claimant of the popular glory. Driven by this hope as if on a raft, he hastened toward Athens, being able to perceive nothing moderate or unworthy concerning himself.
But as he came, in the dark robe as we have said, his immediate business was to walk around the markets and festivals, promising an exhibition of a certain great wisdom, and if anyone wished to become wise according to him, it was easy to make him so in a short time, receiving a small sum of money, and indeed much less than its value. (He was a man of memory, and all that he had previously read he would recite in sequence from memory as if from a book, especially the interpreters of the Aristotelians; but by his "barbarizing" in the flow of his words, it was clear that he was a Latin, for he was no longer recognizable by his beard.)
Toward these things, Nicagoras smiled slightly and said: "Come now, best Xenophanes, tell us what kind of wisdom you have come to demonstrate."
"All-encompassing and varied, Nicagoras, and sufficient for all things, as many and as varied as one might wish to ask."
"Well, let us try, so that they may know, both you with you, and you with these."
But Nicagoras, having judged the magnitude of the promises of Xenophanes, set himself to strike his brow with a certain skill, beginning from the astronomical science. And if he decided to leave aside all the major astronomical hypotheses, whose complex arguments trace out their proofs, and having passed over them, chose to refute him through smaller matters, so that he might be the more put to shame—for the difficulties in the greatest contests do not bring down unadulterated or manifest ridicule, but rather a small and, so to speak, oculary and misty one, and like the gait of Hephaestus, for the most part limping and wavering—but the proofs in the lesser and more ready matters would be a refutation of the ignorance of the bold, very clear and more manifest than the noonday sun. "And how sweet and blessed it is, O Xenophanes," he said, "that you promise to be able to give a demonstration of all wisdom! Come then, take that horoscopic instrument, stand by the sun's ray, and teach us what time it might be at the present moment, so that, having arrived as if from a true point of departure to the finding of the sun and the other stars that happen to be in the zodiac, you may have clear and irrefutable proofs to make of the arguments involved in their motions."
But, alas! That man of the world cannot satisfy even this simple question: he is at a loss at first, and then, recovering himself...


[645] {#pg.149.645}
τὴν ᾿Αριστοτέλους μέντοι φωνὴν, ἀλλ᾿ ὡς ἔχει πάλαι μετενεχθεῖσα ἐς τὴν ἐκείνου συνεσταλμένην γλῶσσαν. Ἐραστὴς δ᾿ ὧν ὁ ἀνὴρ τοῦ φύσει καλοῦ ἐς πόθον ἦκε μεταμαθεῖν καὶ τὴν Ἑλλάδα φωνήν... ὅθεν καὶ μετῆλθε ἐς Καλυδωνίους, ἐκεῖ μετεσκευάσται πάντα, καὶ γλῶτταν, καὶ πώγωνα, καὶ στολήν, καὶ σχῆμα, καὶ ἦθος· οὔτε γὰρ ἔτι κείρεσθαι τὸν πώγωνα δέον ἔχουσιν, οὔτε στολὴν ἐνδεδύσθαι λατινικὴν καὶ οἵ τε θέλεις συμμίγνυσθαι Ἕλλησιν ἑτερόφυλον, ἀλλ᾿ ἵνα μᾶλλον μὴ ἐπιστοῖτο καλύπτεσθαι περ᾿ ὁτωνδήποτε καὶ πρὸς τὸ μελάντερον ὑπὸ τοῦ τριβῶνος. Ἐκεῖθεν μετέβη πρὸς πόλιν Θετταλικήν τινα, σοφίας μᾶλλον ἢ ἀστυγείτονες ἀντιποιουμένην, σταδίους ἀπέχουσαν ᾿Αθηνῶν καὶ τῆς ᾿Αττικῆς οὐ μεῖον ἢ β΄. Ἔνθα συχνὸν περιμείνας χρόνον, ἐναργέστερα εἰς τὴν Ἑλλάδος ἥκει φωνῆς λησμόν... Καλυδῶνε διὰ τὴν τῆς Ἑλλάδος φωνῆς μετάληψιν ἀνυβὰς εἰς ὀφρῦν, μέγαν μὲν ἑαυτὸν εἶναι ἐνόμισε καὶ σοφίας ἁπάσης ἐγκύμονα, τοὺς δ᾿ ἄλλους ὥσπερ ἀπὸ σκοπιᾶς ἦν καθορῶν, εἰς φαῦλόν τινα καὶ κάτω βίον καλινδουμένους. Ἔπει δὲ καὶ τὴν τῆς σοφίας ἁπάσης μητρόπολιν τὰς ᾿Αθήνας ἀθενῶς τηνικαῦτα διοικεῖσθαι περὶ τὰς λόγους ἀκήκοε διὰ τὸ συμβὰν περί τε Μητρόδωρον καὶ Νικαγόραν δυστύχημα καὶ τὴν ἐπώσοπε καταψηφισθεῖσαν αὐτοῦ λήθην καὶ σιωπὴν μᾶλλον ἐρεμνήσει πρὸς τὸ παραδολώτερον τοῦ λογισμοῦ καὶ καιρὸν ἔχειν ἐνθμίσειν ἴση καὶ αὐτὴν ᾿Αθήναζε μηδενὸς τοῦ ἐναντιωσομένου τυγχάνοντος ἀπελθόντα δόξης δημοτικῆς κληροῦχον γενέσθαι. Ταῦτ᾿ ἄρα καὶ ὡς ἐπὶ σχεδίας τινὸς τῆς τοιαύτης ἐλπίδος φερόμενος ἐκώμαζε πρὸς ᾿Αθήνας, οὐδὲν μέτριον οὐδ᾿ ἀγεννὲς συνορᾶν δυνάμενος, περί ἑαυτοῦ.
Là, il a deux buts en vue. L'un c'est de faire argent de sa science (et à cet effet, il annonce un enseignement philosophique merveilleux à bon marché). Iónti γε μὴν ὑπὸ μέλανι ὡς εἰρήκειμεν τῷ τριβῶνι, ἔργον εὐθὺς ἐγαγόνει περιτέναι τὰς ἀγορὰς καὶ τὰς πανηγύρεις, καὶ ἐπαγγέλλεσθαι μεγάλης τινὸς σοφίας ἐπίδειξιν καὶ εἴ τις βούλοιτο σοφὸς κατ᾿ ἐκεῖνον γενέσθαι, ῥᾴδιον εἶναι ποιεῖν αὐτὸν ἐν βραχεῖ, μικρὸν ἀργυρίδιον λαμβάνοντα, καὶ τῆς ἀξίας ἧττον γε πολλῷ. | L'autre c'est de devenir célèbre, et, comme Métrodore était récemment (en 1350, vers le temps de la léthargie d'Andronic) retenu de l'exil et vivait dans Athènes avec Nicagoras, offusqué de leur réputation il se met à déclamer contre eux, et à faire valoir sa supériorité pratique ses idées et méthodes nouvelles. Cette supériorité n'était guère que de la mémoire qui ne le préservait pas de légers barbarismes (μνήμων δ᾿ ἦν ὁ ἀνὴρ, καὶ ὅσα ἦν προανεγνωκὼς πάντα ἀποτάδην ἐκ στόματος διεξῄει καθάπερ ἀπὸ βιβλίου καὶ μάλιστα πάντων σοὺς τῶν ἀριστοτελικῶν ἑρμηνείας · ὑποθαρβαρίζων δὲ τὴν τῶν λέξεων προδὸρ κατάδηλος ἦν ὅτι Λατῖνος ἦν, ἐκ γὰρ τοῦ πώγωνος οὐκ ἦν ἔτι δῆλος.
On comprend très-bien dès lors, comment, à la suite d'incidents sur lesquels nous glissons ici, s'engage un défi d'érudition entre Barlaam et Gré-


[646] {#pg.149.646}
goras, et l'ami de Métrodore. Quand tout est prêt pour cette joûte d'érudition et que le public est assemblé chez Démarate, un des grands personnages d'Athènes, Nicagoras, faisant fonction d'interrogateur, ne tarde pas à établir bien incontestablement que Xénophane, son antagoniste, a dans toute la force du terme, et à la lettre, des prétentions encyclopédiques. Πρὸς ταῦτα βραχὺ μειδιάσας ὁ Νικαγόρας, ῎Αγε δὴ οὖν, βέλτιστε Ξενόφανες, φησὶ, φράζε ἡμῖν τίνα ποτὲ καὶ ὑποδαπὴν τὴν σοφίαν ἔχεις ἐπιδειξόμενος. Παντοδαπὴν καὶ ποικίλην, ὦ Νικαγόρα, καὶ πρὸς πάντα ἀρκέσανσαν, ὁπόσα καὶ οἷα ἄν ἐρωτᾶν βούλοιτο · τῷ δὲ μὴν πειρῆσαι, ἵνα γνώωσι καὶ οἶδε σὺν σοὶ, καὶ σὺν τοῖσδε αὐ. Après ce vers d'Homère, et une fois bien acquis les paroles mêmes de Xénophane, qu'il prétend n'ignorer de rien (de telle sorte que s'il se trouve incapable de répondre sur les principes et problèmes usuels de telle ou telle science, évidemment il aura encouru le reproche ou de charlatanisme ou de légèreté, si ce n'est de l'un et de l'autre), Grégoras, que nous savons avoir été pourvu de connaissances astronomiques et en avoir été très-vain, entame l'examen par l'astronomie et prie l'Italien de déterminer astronomiquement (par l'horaire) l'heure du jour, se réservant de le faire passer de ce point de départ à la détermination du lieu du soleil et des planètes sous le zodiaque ; et il a bien soin de nous avertir que la question unique qu'il pose pour le moment est de celles qu'on adresse aux élèves en astronomie :
Ὁ μέντοι Νικαγόρας τοῦ μεγέθους καταγνοὺς τὰς ἐπαγγελίας τοῦ Ξενόφανους, δεξιῶς πως ἐπιῤῥαπίζειν αὐτοῦ τὴν ὀφρῦν ἐγχειρίχειν ἐκ τῆς ἀστρονομικῆς ἐπιστήμης ἀρξάμενος. Καὶ εἰ δὴ λοιπὸν τὰς τῶν ἀστρονομικῶν ὑποθέσεων μείζους καὶ ὧν λέγει ποικίλοι τὰς ἀποδείξεις ἰχνηλατοῦσι· πάσας ὄψεις καὶ παραδραμὼν διὰ τῶν μικροτέρων ἔκρινε δεῖν ἐξελέγχειν αὐτον, ἵνα μάλα μᾶλλον αἰσχύνοιντο · τὰς γὰρ ἂν τοῖς μεγίστοις ἀγῶσιν ἀμηχανίας οὐδ᾿ ἄκρατον οὐδ᾿ ἐμφανῆ τὴν χλεύην ἐπιστρέσθαι, ἀλλὰ μικράν τινα καὶ ὡς εἰπεῖν ὀφθαλμίοσαν καὶ ὀμιχλώδη, καὶ κατὰ τὰς Ἡφαίστου πορείας καὶ ταύτην ὡς τῇ πολλῷ χωλεύουσαν τε καὶ ἐπαμφοτερίζουσαν, τὰς δ᾿ ἐν τοῖς ἥττοσι καὶ προχείροις, ταύτας δ᾿ ἔλεγχον γίνεσθαι τῆς τῶν θρασυτέρων ἀμαθίας μάλα σαφῆ καὶ μεσημβρινῆς ἀκτῖνος περιφανέστερον. Καὶ Ὡς ἡδὺς εἰ καὶ μακάριος, ὦ Ξενόφανες, ἔφησεν, οὕτω περὶ πάσης σοφίας ἐπίδειξιν ἱκανὸς εἶναι ἐπαγγελλόμενος. Φέρε τοίνυν, τὸν ὡροσκόπον ἐκεῖνον ἀεξάμενος, στῆθι παρὰ τὴν τοῦ ἡλίου ἀκτῖνα καὶ τὴν ὥραν ἥτις πυτὲ εἴη ἐν γε τῷ παρόντι δίδαξον, ἵνα ὡς ἐξ ὁρμητηρίου τινὸς ἀληθοῦς ἀφικόμενος ἐς τὴν εὕρεσιν τοῦ τε ἡλίου καὶ τῶν ἄλλων ἀστέρων ὅπῃ τοῦ ζωδιακοῦ τυγχάνουσιν ὄντες ἕκαστος ἔχῃς σαφεῖς καὶ ἀναντιῤῥήτους τὰς ἀποδείξεις ποιεῖσθαι τῶν ἐμπεριλημμένων ταῖς τούτων κινήσεσι λόγων. — Mais, hélas ! cette question si simple, l'homme universel n'y peut satisfaire : il reste court d'abord, puis se ranimant, il
